Observationally Equivalent Algorithms:
What Prices Reveal About Trader Populations,
and What Supervisors Need
Pratik Niroula∗ Prashant Niroula † Sujan Khatiwada‡
14 November 2026
Revised research draft for author review
Abstract
An exchange can reduce feedback trading across a market or restrict a particular class
of traders. We study what the public record reveals about the effects of these actions.
For traders who follow a common public signal, the aggregate feedback response de-
termines public outcomes under fixed causal strategies. A uniform reduction depends
on this response; a class-specific restriction can meet a prudential ceiling in one popu-
lation and fail in another with nearly indistinguishable public records. For a smooth
one-parameter family, common initialization and Gaussian innovations, we construct
K-type populations within radius ∆ whose distinction requires at least order ∆−(4K−2)
observations under a uniform derivative-moment bound. For separated linear-memory
classes, a full-rank derivative condition yields matching recovery from noisy determin-
istic responses. Class-tagged flows remove the spacing penalty for recovering weights
once at most one memory class remains untagged. A Gaussian identity relates pairwise
detection to forecast mismatch. A Bayesian predictor has a uniform cumulative log-
loss bound under fixed stability and memory margins. We distinguish these theoretical
results from the pending empirical tests and from the portions checked in Lean 4.
AI-use disclosure and status.AI systems assisted with research, drafting, code and formal
checks. This revision corrects the scope of the smooth-family and forecasting results and adds
proofs and validation material. Human author approval and independent referee review of this
revision are pending. Lean certifies the stated formal declarations; it does not certify empirical
validity, novelty or full manuscript coverage.

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
1 Introduction
The economic question.A supervisor may know how strongly a market reacts to past
prices while knowing little about which traders generate that response. These are different
objects of inference. If every group’s feedback is scaled by the same factor, the aggregate
response is sufficient to evaluate the intervention in our model. If a particular group is
halted, its contribution matters. This paper quantifies that distinction and the information
supplied by class-tagged reports.
The answer.Under a common public signal and fixed causal rules, the population enters
public market outcomes through its aggregate flow map. For linear rules this map is the
feedback kernel. Equal kernels give equal public paths, insider profits and dealer inventories
under the same shocks and strategies; the resulting equilibrium correspondence uses the same
strategy spaces and public payoff maps. Individual feedback traders’ payoffs need not agree.
A counterfactual that changes their strategies requires a separate invariance or equilibrium
argument.
Three results organize the analysis. First, closely spaced types are weakly identified. A
moment-matched pair ofK-type populations has aggregate-flow differences of order ∆ 2K−1,
leading to a Gaussian information lower bound of order ∆ −(4K−2). The smooth-family re-
sult requires common initial information, predictable probes and a derivative-moment bound
uniform over the experiments being compared. It is a worst-case lower bound; some smooth
families are never identified. Second, aggregate feedback can suffice for a decision even when
the underlying population is hard to estimate. Proposition 6.1 gives the intervention com-
parison, and Corollary 6.2 expresses its consequence as expected decision loss. Third, reports
that separately measure class flows change the observation experiment. In the exponential-
memory model, tagging all but one memory class removes the spacing penalty for recovering
class weights. An additional tag can still improve precision.
A forecasting benchmark.If the common pricing-error signal is observed, known pa-
rameters and zero initial group states make current positions reconstructible from its history.
For an unknown fixed population in a stationary Gaussian market, a specified autoregressive
predictor has excess forecast risk of order logT/Tpointwise. This separates estimating an
aggregate response from estimating its components. Theorem 5.3 adds a uniform cumulative
log-loss bound under zero initialization and known Gaussian innovation variance. Neither re-
sult establishes a monotone decline in the value of positions or irrelevance for traded returns
with hidden fundamentals. Theorem 5.1 relates pairwise detection to forecast mismatch,
1

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
while Theorem 4.7 proves a recovery bound within separated shrinking clusters in a specified
response experiment.
Results and interpretation.Theorems 3.1–3.2 identify the sufficient public object; The-
orems 4.1–4.5 quantify identification costs in their specified experiments. Theorem 4.2 clas-
sifies linear population functionals by their estimation cost. Section 6 studies common in-
terventions, targeting, partial disclosure and pooling across markets with shared memory
rates. Section 7 considers bounded drift, and Section 8 separates calm observations from
observations that reach hard position limits. Illustrative calendar-year conversions depend
on assumed noise, weight and spacing parameters; they are not estimates of a particular
venue’s identification horizon.
The motivation is consistent with evidence of difficult parameter estimation in
heterogeneous-agent models (Lux and Zwinkels, 2018) and weak predictive content of
large-trader positions in ten agricultural futures markets (Sanders et al., 2009). Those
findings neither identify the mechanism here nor test its intervention counterfactuals. The
empirical exercises are reported with their registered outcomes and limitations.
What is known.Exponential memory is the oldest model of expectations: adaptive ex-
pectations (Cagan, 1956; Nerlove, 1958), geometric distributed lags (Koyck, 1954), and Muth
(1960)’s proof that exponentially weighted forecasts are optimal for a random walk observed
with noise. The idea that an aggregate depends on a distribution of individual coefficients
and not on the individuals is also old. Granger (1980) showed it for sums of autoregressions,
Forni and Lippi (1997) developed the general theory of aggregation and dynamics, Zaffaroni
(2004) and Pesaran and Chudik (2014) the large-economy and large-panel versions, and re-
covering the distribution is the disaggregation problem (Celov et al., 2010). Results of the
form “two structures give the same data, so data cannot choose” have a famous precedent
in Sargent (1976), whose observational equivalence of natural and unnatural rate theories
settled what reduced-form data could say about monetary rules. A large literature models
markets with heterogeneous fundamentalist and chartist traders (Brock and Hommes, 1998;
Hommes, 2006) and estimates their shares from prices (Boswijk et al., 2007; Franke and
Westerhoff, 2012; Lux and Zwinkels, 2018); our results say which features of a population
such estimates can recover from prices, and at what cost, when demand is linear in past
prices. Auclert et al. (2024) make the corresponding sufficient-statistic argument for house-
hold heterogeneity in macroeconomics. That a decision needs only a reduction of the data
is the subject of sufficiency and the comparison of experiments (Blackwell, 1953; Le Cam,
1964), and control theory distinguishes identifying a system from having data that are in-
2

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
formative enough for a control objective (van Waarde et al., 2020). The rate (∆η) −(4K−2) is
the known minimax rate for mixtures with near-colliding components (Heinrich and Kahn,
2018; Wu and Yang, 2020), and the uniqueness step behind the stress result is the unique-
ness of networks with one hidden layer (Sussmann, 1992). The exponent 2K−1 in the gap
between near-colliding populations is also the error amplification for super-resolution ofK
near-colliding point sources (Batenkov et al., 2021), of which Prony’s method is the classical
instance. That disaggregate information helps forecast an aggregate when the components
have different dynamics is known from the literature on forecasting aggregates (L¨ utkepohl,
1987; Hendry and Hubrich, 2011); Proposition 5.5(b) is that result in this market. We do
not claim these.
Trader identity in market microstructure.Identity disclosure already has an estab-
lished information role. Linnainmaa and Saar (2012) study investor information inferred
from broker identity and order flow. Frino et al. (2026) examine delayed identity disclo-
sure and price informativeness with informed traders. Those mechanisms differ from the
common-public-signal benchmark here, and show why a general irrelevance claim would be
inappropriate. Our intervention comparison concerns estimating response coefficients and
assigning restrictions, rather than establishing that identity can have value for price discov-
ery.
Recent spectral-estimation work also develops single- and multi-snapshot stability
bounds, including Huang and Liu (2026). Its stated Fourier-measurement and noise as-
sumptions differ from the feedback-market experiments here. We cite its existence and
scope rather than infer a new statistical priority claim from the market application.
Contribution relative to existing methods.The statistical exponent and moment-
matching method have close antecedents in finite-mixture estimation and super-resolution.
Our contribution is their use in an endogenous feedback market with explicit observation
contracts, combined with a comparison of common and class-specific interventions. Suffi-
ciency is pathwise under common causal strategies; the price response identifies memory
rates as transmission zeros under the mechanical-impact model. Partial disclosure changes
the number of untagged classes, pooling uses shared rates with diverse market weights, and
hard limits separate calm-path equivalence from stress identification. These are conditional
market results. Local numerical rank checks do not establish global identification, and a
lower bound alone does not provide an attainable rate for every smooth family.

2 Model
Time is discrete. Letx t be the pricing error, the gap between the price and the fundamental
value. A reduced-form dealer sector absorbs net order flow with price impactκ >0, and
arbitrage pulls the error toward zero with persistencea∈[0,1). There areKgroups of
feedback traders, with 0<η i <1 unless a boundary case is explicitly stated. Groupikeeps

Observed signal.In the reduced-form results the observer seesx t, or a public signal from
which it is reconstructible. If fundamental value is hidden, traded prices alone need not
reveal pricing errors. Section 5 separates these observation regimes. The dynamic sufficiency
theorem uses a signal explicitly measurable from public history.
The observer.The cost results concern a supervisor or exchange that knows (a,κ) and
the noise law. It can wait and record prices, probe from a reset state and record the response,
probe the running market with random signs (which costs the same order of data, Supplement
Theorem S16.1), or receive class-tagged flow reports. Reset probing is a benchmark for what
data can reveal, not a proposed policy. Knowing (a,κ) only helps the observer, so the lower
bounds below hold a fortiori when (a,κ) must be estimated.
When impact is mechanical.Model (1) lets feedback flow move the price. That de-
scribes markets in which prices are set by the mechanics of a limit order book or an automated
4

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
market maker, or by dealers who price flow by its effect on their inventory (Supplement The-
orem S3.1). It does not describe a market maker who sets prices as conditional expectations
of value and knows the aggregate flow rule. Feedback flow is then predictable from past
prices, is netted out and moves no price, so prices are those of a market without feed-
back traders and reveal nothing about them (Companion Proposition C2.1(iv)). Sufficiency
(Theorem 3.1) holds for any dealer rule. The identification and cost results are stated for
mechanical impact; the barrier they describe is not an artefact of it, since with a fully rational
market maker prices reveal less, not more.
3 The kernel is sufficient and is what prices identify
For sufficiency we embed the groups in a general market. A valuevis drawn, noise traders
submitz t, an insider submitsX t(v,Ht−1), and a dealer sets the priceP t(Ht−1,yt) from
the public history and the total order flowy t. The groups update on a public signal
st =S t(p0,...,p t) and contribute flowf t = ∑m<tkmst−1−m. The mapsX,PandSare
arbitrary causal rules. The strategic players in the equivalence below are the insider and
dealer; feedback-group rules are fixed and their individual payoffs are not part of the payoff
equivalence.
Theorem 3.1(The kernel is sufficient).If two group configurations havek m =k ′m for
m<T, then for every strategy profile and every realization of(v,z)the price and order-flow
paths, the insider’s trades and realized profit, and the dealer’s inventory coincide on dates
t≤T. The two games have the same strategy sets and payoffs, and hence the same equilibria
under any concept defined from payoffs and the public history. The same holds for any finite
population whose flows are causal linear functions of the public signal, withkthe sum of the
individual kernels. Group-level quantities, such as the weight of the largest group, are not
invariant.
The proof is an induction on dates (Supplement Theorem S2.1 and Proposition S2.2). It
is short; its content is the object it isolates, a population-level quantity that settles every
equilibrium concept at once. The induction uses only that aggregate feedback flow is a
fixed causal function of the public signal. So for any population, linear or not, including
traders who switch strategies after observing past profits, the aggregate flow map is sufficient
(machine-checked in this generality; Supplement Theorem S6.1); the kernel is that map when
flows are linear. Equal kernels remain observationally equivalent when anonymous aggregate
flow is added to the public record. This does not imply that flow reports cannot improve
finite-sample precision or reveal more than prices under another dealer rule. For example, a
5

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
dealer that always quotes zero hides every kernel in prices, while a deterministic public signal
equal to one makes the first aggregate feedback flow revealk 0. The converse identification
statement below needs the specified mechanical-impact experiment.
Theorem 3.2(What prices identify).In model(1), fix common known(a,κ)and zero initial
group states, and compare noiseless probe responses. Two economies produce identical price
responses to every probe sequence if and only if their merged measures coincide.
This is Supplement Theorem S2.3(i). The same holds whenκandaare derived from
competitive dealers who dislike inventory: the equilibrium price follows (1) path by path
with effective impact and persistence, and with the memory rates unchanged (Supplement
Theorems S3.1 and S3.2).
Together, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 make the kernel a minimal identifying functional of the
population under the full-response experiment. It is a reduction of the structural parameter,
not a statistic computed from a finite data sample in the Fisher–Neyman sense. Finite noisy
passive records require the separate excitation, noise and estimation assumptions below.
Any population.For any population, linear or not, probe responses identify exactly the
aggregate flow map, and a group-level quantity is identified if and only if it is a function of
that map (Supplement Theorem S6.7, machine-checked for an arbitrary flow map). Theo-
rem 3.2 is the linear case. The lower bounds of the next section hold over any class of rules
that contains the linear ones, so richer rules cannot make composition cheaper to learn.
Proposition 3.3(What an intervention needs).Let observations depend on the population
only through its aggregate flow map, as prices and probe responses do.
(i) If the loss from an action depends on the population only through the flow map, the set
of safe actions is a function of what prices identify.
(ii) If two populations have the same flow map and an action is safe in one and unsafe in
the other, every rule based on observations takes the action with the same probability
in both.
(iii) If the records of the two populations are within total-variation distanceδ, a rule that
takes the action with probabilitypwhere it is safe takes it with probability at leastp−δ
where it is unsafe.
Parts (i) and (ii) restate Theorem 3.1, and part (iii) is the definition of total variation.
The proposition is the decision form of sufficiency (Blackwell, 1953; Le Cam, 1964); we
state it because it organizes what follows. A uniform cut in feedback is case (i) and a halt
6

of the largest group is case (ii) (Proposition 6.1). Theorem 4.1 boundsδin case (iii) for
composition: withNrecords,δ≤C

N(∆η) 2K−1/σ.
4 The price of composition
Theorem 3.2 says that groups with different memories can be told apart in principle. The
question is how much data that takes when memories are close.
Theorem 4.1(The cost of composition).FixK≥2, a center rater c, a total weightWand
a spacing∆η, with every candidate economy stable. Each reset experiment records the noisy
impulse response with Gaussian noise of varianceσ 2.
(i)Lower bound.There are twoK-group economies with rates inr c + ∆η[−1,1]and
positive weights whose merged measures have the same moments of order0,...,2K−2
and whose impulse responses differ by order(∆η) 2K−1. Telling them apart with error
probability below1/4needsN≥cσ 2(∆η)−(4K−2) experiments.
(ii)Upper bound.On any class of separated rescaled rates with weights bounded away
from zero, Prony’s method applied to the first2Kestimated kernel values recovers the
weights fromN=Cσ 2(∆η)−(4K−2) experiments.
(iii)Waiting is no cheaper.If prices are instead observed passively, driven by Gaussian
news, the information per observation between the pair in (i) is of order(∆η) 4K−2, so
at least of order(∆η) −(4K−2) observations are needed.
Parts (i)–(iii) are Supplement Theorems S4.1, S4.2 and S4.4. Part (iii) does not weaken
as the market approaches instability (Proposition 4.4). For two groups, halving the spread
of memories multiplies the data needed by 64; for three groups, by 1,024. The pair in (i)
consists of the Gauss and Gauss–Radau quadrature rules, which match moments as far asK
nodes allow. Part (iii) uses that the memory rates are zeros of the closed-loop response, so
passive data face the same near-cancellation as probes. Gaussian news is the least favourable
case: for any news law whose location divergence is quadratically bounded, the divergence
is at mostσ 2I∗f times the Gaussian one, so the order in (iii) is unchanged (Supplement
Proposition S16.2;σ 2I∗f = 2 for Laplace news).
Four extensions.Without resets.A supervisor that trades random signs into a market
running from an unknown state, and correlates prices with its own trades, estimates the
response with error of orderT −1/2 whatever the law of the news; Prony’s method then
recovers the weights from order (∆η)−(4K−2) periods, and no method needs fewer (Supplement
7

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
Theorem S16.1).Without separation.If groups may nearly coincide or be tiny, individual
weights cannot be recovered uniformly, but the merged memory distribution can, at the same
orderN −1/(4K−2) in Wasserstein distance (Supplement Theorem S19.1).General memory.If
memory is any mixture of exponentials, finite or infinite, composition is harder still: the best
attainable accuracy for the memory distribution improves only like 1/log(N/σ 2), and this
order is attained, so halving the error squares the data needed (Supplement Theorem S18.2;
both bounds are machine-checked, with larger constants).Money.Probing a market is
trading in it: to tell one group from two with memories±δapart, the quantity traded must
grow likeδ−2 and the capital at risk likeδ −4 (Supplement Theorem S10.1 and Section S10).
Priced this way, the cost of learning composition from prices is a lower bound on the money
value of class tags to a supervisor who needs composition (Supplement Corollary S16.3).

Corollary 4.3(Identification and market-wide questions separate).FixK≥2, an accuracy
ϵand a cheap questionF g as in Theorem 4.2(ii). OverK-group populations whose memo-
ries lie within∆η, the number of experiments needed to learn the weights to within a fixed
fraction of total weight, divided by the number needed to answerF g to accuracyϵ, is at least
cϵ 2(∆η)−(4K−2). It tends to infinity as∆η→0.
The corollary combines Theorem 4.1(i), whose two economies have weights that differ
by a fixed fraction of the total, with Theorem 4.2(ii), whose cost does not depend on the
spacing. The same holds, with a logarithm, for the total feedback weight. In a Monte Carlo
with two groups, the error of the discounted feedback does not move with the spacing of
memories (fitted slope−0.02), while the error of the weights grows like its inverse cube (slope
−3.02).
Proposition 4.4(The barrier does not lift near instability).Hold the pair of Theorem 4.1(i)
at a common feedback budgetS= 1−ϵ. The passive information per observation between
them is at mostC(∆η) 4K−2, withCindependent ofϵ, however close the market is to insta-
bility.
This is Supplement Proposition S4.5. One might expect a market near instability to
reveal its traders, since the loop amplifies every difference. It reveals instead how close it is to
instability: with a common persistence rather than a common budget, the information grows
without bound asϵ→0, and all of the growth comes from the budget. The bound is per
observation, and it extends to finite samples uniformly inϵ(Supplement Proposition S12.5);
in every case computed the exact information innobservations lies within 3% belowntimes
the per-observation rate (Supplement Section S12.2). Figure 1 shows both statements.
How large is the barrier?Table 1 evaluates the lower bound for particular moment-
matched pairs under the listed parameter values and passive Gaussian observation contract.
At the illustrative half-lives of 5–9 periods and a daily sampling convention, some pairs
require more than 4,000 calendar years to distinguish at the stated error level. This is
a worst-case example, not a bound for every population in that range or a fitted market
horizon. Smaller lower bounds for more separated pairs do not by themselves establish that
the associated sample size is sufficient. Regular non-Gaussian innovation families can retain
the exponent under the location-divergence assumptions of Supplement Proposition S16.2;
bounded-support laws are outside that claim. Synthetic groups driven by resampled Bitcoin
noise provide an additional numerical check, not a theorem for arbitrary real-market noise.

The barrier does not depend on exponential memory.Exponential memory makes
the problem explicit, but the exponent has a more general source. Let each type of trader
be indexed by a numberθin an interval Θ and tradef θ(h) after the public historyh=
(xt,xt−1,...), wheref θ is any causal rule: a power-law or hump-shaped memory, a fixed
lookback window, a saturating rule, or a learning rule with a fixed tuning parameter. A
population is a finite measureνon Θ, the weight on each type, and its aggregate flow
Fν(h) =

is linear inνwhatever the rules. Prices follow (1) withκF ν(ht−1) in place of the feedback
term. Theorem 3.1 says thatF ν is all that prices can reveal; the next result says what it
costs to readνfrom it.

The inverse inequality is an additional assumption. A local derivative-rank calculation

gap simulations support the predicted Taylor orders in three non-exponential examples. A
revised high-precision calculation checks efficient information for one weight by projecting
its derivative off the nuisance derivatives. Finite-design numerical evidence is distinguished
from the inverse assumption.

5 Forecasting the public signal without recovering
composition
The forecasting target is the pricing errorx t in (1). We assume it is observed by the
forecaster. Observing a traded pricep t =x t +f t alone does not generally revealx t. The
forecast benchmarks takeq i≥0, 0< r i <1,κ >0,S <1 and no probes, with boundary
cases stated explicitly. The pairwise detection comparison separately allows predictable
probes. The results use different initialization and decision regimes.
Theorem 5.1(Pairwise detection and forecast mismatch).Consider two specified causal
aggregate mapsF,F ′ with the same knowna,κ, common observed initial information, in-
dependentN(0,σ 2)innovations, and a common predictable probe policy including the same
independent randomization. Assume the required squared mean gaps are integrable. For a
record ofTsubsequent innovations, let

Both testing errors can be below1/4only ifΛ T >1. For a countable set of candidate
record densities with prior massπ ∗ >0on the truth, the Bayesian mixture also satisfies the
one-sided pathwise bound

This is the Gaussian chain rule and the standard mixture likelihood bound, applied to
the market experiment (Dawid, 1984; Vovk, 2001). It identifies a pairwise forecast mismatch
with a directional information divergence. It does not identify total information about an
unrestricted population, prove that every kernel is cheaply estimated, or make a failed detec-
tion test evidence of a small KL divergence. The test condition is necessary, not sufficient.
The mixture bound is an upper bound on regret, not an absolute difference of log scores; it
requires positive prior mass on the true candidate.

squared-error forecast. Its predictive variance incorporates parameter uncertainty. Unknown

the bound need not remain small as ¯sor ¯rapproaches one. The Gaussian integral calculation
in Supplement Section S30 proves the bound directly.
Remark5.4 (Drift remains a separate problem).For weights changing over time, the preced-
ing stationary expansion does not apply. A window-length trade-off suggests rates involving
the drift speed, but requires mixing, stability, excitation and estimation bounds uniform over
the drifting class. The earlier simulation selected a window by its realized evaluation error
and used random-walk increments, rather than a deterministic bound on each increment. It
is exploratory and supplies no proof of a bounded-drift rate. A valid test fixes the window
using training data and evaluates independent seeds without selecting on their outcomes.
Proposition 5.5(Channels through which class records can matter).(a)Private sig-
nals.There are specified-flow economies in which a tagged record reveals future news
absent from the public history and reduces forecast risk by a positive amount. This is
an existence statement, without an optimal-trading or equilibrium claim.
(b)Exogenous component flows.Suppose both observers know the feedback parameters
and recoverable states and observe the exact aggregate exogenous flowU s = ∑iui,s
for everys≤t. The augmented observer also sees everyu i,s. Let these flows be
jointly stationary Gaussian and independent of price news, and assume price initial-
ization adds no information about future flows conditional on their histories. For
xt+1 =ax t +κ ∑iqiyi,t +κUt+1 +εt+1, the reduction in forecast risk is exactlyκ 2 times
the reduction in projection-error variance forU t+1. It is zero if all components have
common autoregressive coefficients and innovations unpredictable from the joint past.
(c)Unobserved components of the price.If onlyp t =x t +ft is observed, a class record can
help inferx t and improve forecasts ofp t+1 without advance information about future
news.

(d)Stress and interventions.The hard-limit experiment of Theorem 8.1 and the targeted
decision of Proposition 6.1 concern further sources of value. The exact calm-market
result is for band-linear hard limits. Smooth saturation need not be exactly invisible in
noiseless small-probe observations. These channels are not exhaustive.
The aggregate-versus-component forecasting comparison is established in the disaggrega-
tion literature (L¨ utkepohl, 1987; Hendry and Hubrich, 2011); the benchmark here specifies
when feedback components add no information. Supplement Section S28 proves the claims
and gives explicit counterexamples to broader interpretations.
Proposition 5.6(Fitted-forecast gains and the value of information).LetDbe the training
sigma field. LetF B ⊆F A be the baseline and augmented forecast information sets, both
containingD, and letm j =E[Y|F j]for a square-integrable targetY. For square-integrable

Thus an upper bound on the gain of fitted forecasts bounds the oracle value of additional
information only with a separate bound on the augmented predictor’s excess risk. Dividing
by a common positive variance normalization gives the corresponding identity for normalized
gains.
This projection identity is standard; its role here is to delimit what the proposed forecast
exercise can establish. In an offline diagnostic with a known population-oracle gain of 0.005,
the candidate classified 55 of 200 iid worlds as irrelevant, with 133 inconclusive and 12
relevant. Its average estimated gain was 0.004688. The diagnostic failed its stated boundary
criterion. It evaluates the population-oracle boundary, which differs from the conditional
fitted-predictor estimand; the discrepancy therefore does not by itself isolate a bootstrap
failure. We report it as a reason to require an estimand-specific calibration before live
inference, rather than as a market result or a test of Theorem 5.2.
Empirical scope.P1F compares specified forecasts of traded returns with and without
Binance position-ratio features. The ratios are neither class-tagged executed flows nor the
model’s observed pricing error. A favorable result would support that forecast compari-
son within its power and equivalence margins; it would not establish structural invisibility
or identify the mechanism. The first window was void: zero of 30 symbols met the regis-
tered coverage rule and no forecast outcome was computed. The replacement window is 16

6 Who needs composition, and what disclosure buys
Proposition 6.1(Market-wide versus targeted action).Letq i≥0, let the market be stable

(ii)Targeted.Halting the largest group does not. For everyK≥2there are two popula-
tions whose merged measures have the same moments of order0,...,2K−2, so that
their budgets differ only by order(∆η) 2K−1, while their largest groups differ by a fixed
amount, so that for a range of ceilings the halt meets the ceiling in one and not the
other. Making this decision fromNreset records with both errors at most1/4needs
N≥cσ 2(∆η)−(4K−2).
Part (i) is Supplement Proposition S5.1(i). Part (ii) is Supplement Proposition S12.4; its
pair comes from a family of quadrature rules whose weights are the values of one function
at their nodes (Supplement Lemma S12.3).

Theorem 6.3(Value of class-tagged disclosure).Suppose that, in addition to prices, the
observer receives each class’s flow with Gaussian measurement noise. On the rich class of
Theorem 4.1(ii), the number of experiments needed to recover the weights falls from order
(∆η)−(4K−2) to a bound that does not depend on∆η. For the rates it falls to order(∆η) −2.
This is Supplement Theorem S5.3; Supplement Proposition S5.2 gives the regression that
achieves it, one class at a time, with a variance that depends only on that class. Supplement
19

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
Proposition S27.3 says when a supervisor should pay for such an audit trail: when the
algorithms to be told apart are close enough that prices take too long, but not so close that
even tagged data cannot help, and when the trail covers enough markets to recover its fixed
cost.
Real regimes tag some traders and leave the rest anonymous. The next result prices that.
Theorem 6.4(Partial disclosure).For recovery of weights to fixed accuracy under the sup-
plement’s rich-class conditions, if the flows ofmof theKgroups are tagged, the cost is that
of theK ′ =K−muntagged groups alone: of order(∆η) −(4K′−2) experiments ifK ′≥2, and
a number that does not depend on∆ηifK ′≤1.
This is Supplement Theorem S5.4; the lower bound uses theK ′-node Gauss and Gauss–
Radau pair with the tagged groups held fixed, and the upper bound subtracts the tagged
groups’ estimated contribution and applies Prony’s method to the rest. Each tag lowers the
exponent by four. The tag that leaves a single group untagged is worth the most, a factor
of order (∆η)−6, and the final tag leaves the leading spacing exponent for weight recovery
unchanged. An additional tag can improve constants and decision precision, so its exact
value is not zero in general. Tagging all but one memory class removes the spacing penalty
for weight recovery under this observation contract. What matters is how many distinct
memory classes stay untagged, not how many traders. A regime that tags registered high-
speed traders gets the full benefit only if everyone else behaves, in memory, like a single
class.
A supervisor who sees several markets has a second route that needs no tags at all. The
pooling theorem assumes that an algorithm has the same memory across markets while its
participation varies. This is a restriction to test, not a property of every algorithm.
Theorem 6.5(Pooling markets).Let the sameKalgorithms trade inDmarkets, with
common memory rates and market-specific weights whose matrix has rankD≤K. Pool-
ing the price records of theDmarkets, the data needed to learn composition are of order
(∆η)−2(K−1+⌈K/D⌉) near generic configurations:(∆η) −(4K−2) with one market and(∆η) −2K
withKor more. Over a compact class the exponent lies between2(K−1 +⌈K/D⌉)and
4K−2, so pooling never needs more data than one market; whether it equals the local expo-
nent is a global question that is open in general.
Supplement Proposition S5.6 adds a block-Hankel rank certificate: it gives global identi-
fication at this moment order, holds generically, and yields the same global rate on compact
classes satisfying the certificate throughout. Rank-deficient exceptions and noisy rank deci-
sions remain distinct issues.
20

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
This is Supplement Theorem S5.5. The lower bound on the joint records of all markets
is machine-checked for any matched pair, and so is the upper bound given the rank condi-
tion, which holds at almost every configuration for any number of algorithms and markets
(machine-checked at an explicit configuration); Monte Carlo runs of the estimator reproduce
the predicted rates. Cross-market surveillance is a partial substitute for tagging, and the
converse constrains hiding: to stay as hidden as in a single market, the algorithms must
trade in the same proportions everywhere. For two algorithms with half-lives between 5 and
9 days, about 7,000 years of daily prices in one market become at least about 90 in two
markets in which their shares differ by 50 percentage points (hardest pair found numerically;
Supplement Section S5.3).
Two further results say what the supervisor cannot use instead. Markets with the same
eigenvalues, the same one-step and long-run price variance can have arbitrarily different
worst-case short-run gains, so no monitor built on eigenvalues and second moments of prices
is uniformly safe (Supplement Theorem S26.1). And what prices hide is economically spe-
cific: for desks that track a trend with Kalman filters, prices depend on the desks only
through their signal-to-noise ratios and intensities, so how good each desk’s information is,
the thing a supervisor would most like to know, is what costs (∆η) −(4K−2) to learn (Supple-
ment Propositions S23.1 and S23.2).
The results so far take the groups’ rules as given. A group that expects to be targeted
has a reason to change them.
Proposition 6.6(Hiding caps targeting).A supervisor observes prices, under any probes
and for any record length, and then targets a given group or not. Being targeted costs the
groupP >0. Suppose the group can adopt, at private costC 0, a rule that gives the population
the kernel of a population that the supervisor targets with probabilityα. Then in any best
response the group is targeted with probability at mostα+C 0/P, whatever the probes, record
length and decision rule.
The proof is two lines from Theorem 3.1 (Supplement Proposition S5.7). More data make
the group’s original rule easier to detect and so make hiding more attractive. With several
groups, the bound applies to each group’s best response given the others’ rules, so it holds
group by group in any equilibrium; what determines each group’sαis not modelled. A class
tag is attached to the account, so changing the trading rule does not remove it.
21

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
7 Surveillance over time
Populations drift: algorithms enter, exit and are retuned. Suppose the population moves by
at mostvper period in its rescaled memories and weights, and the supervisor runsnreset
experiments per period.
Theorem 7.1(A speed limit on surveillance).WithΓ =σ 2v(∆η)−(4K−2)/n, the best at-
tainable error in tracking the weights is of ordermin(1,Γ 1/3): no estimator does better along
some admissible path, and Prony’s method on a moving window of the right length attains it.
Ifvexceeds a speed limit of ordern(∆η) 4K−2/σ2, no record of any length tracks the weights
at all. A linear question with square-summable coefficients whose answer moves by at most
vF per period is tracked with error of order(σ 2vF/n)1/3, whatever the number of groups and
the spacing of their memories.
This is Supplement Theorem S15.1. Composition is untrackable unless it is stable over
the time it takes to identify it, which at illustrative parameters is thousands of years of daily
prices; market-wide quantities are tracked at the cube-root rate. In a simulation with two
groups moving along the hard family, the weight error grows with speed and spacing at fitted
slopes 0.33 and−2.01, against 1/3 and−2.
Two strategic responses sharpen the point. If traders adjust their rules in response to what
they earn, a probe of sizeAbuys information at rateA 2 and moves its target at rateA 2; for
any class of learning rules whose response to probes is bounded byγbut otherwise unknown,
every probing policy leaves a tracking error of at least order min(1,(σ 2γ(∆η)−(4K−2))1/3)
(Supplement Proposition S15.2). And a group that expects to be targeted once identified
can move its memory toward its neighbour’s. In a two-group game in which that move costs
k(D0−D) 2 and detection costsL, more data without tags push the group’s memory toward
its neighbour’s, and its detection probability never exceeds the false-alarm rate pluskD 20/L,
however much data the supervisor has; tags remove the incentive to crowd (Supplement
Proposition S15.3). Where algorithm identification is introduced, the dispersion of memories
across algorithm classes should therefore rise, a prediction that only algorithm-level data can
test.
8 Position limits: calm hides, stress reveals
Real feedback traders have position limits. Let traderihave memory rater i, capacityc i >0
and leverageℓ i, and tradec iψ(ℓimi,t), wherem i,t is its exponentially weighted signal andψis
a shape common to all traders. The leading case is the hard limitψ(u) = max(−1,min(1,u)):
22

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
inside its band a trader is linear with weightc iℓi; at the edge its position is capped. Write
ν= ∑iciδ(ri,ℓi) for the capacity measure on memory and leverage.
Theorem 8.1(Two layers).(i)Sufficiency.For any shape, populations with the sameν
give the same prices, profits and equilibria. The kernel is one linear functional ofν,
km =
∫
rmℓdν, and does not determine it.
(ii)Calm hides.Under hard limits, two populations with the same kernel give identical price
and order-flow paths until some trader first reaches its limit. Any record of the market
before dateTdistinguishes them with total-variation distance at most the probability
that a limit is reached beforeT. With Gaussian news and no probe this is at most
T(n+n ′)e−1/(2ϑ2) for then+n ′ traders of the two populations, whereϑis the largest
standard deviation of a trader’s position relative to its limit.
(iii)Stress reveals.If every trader hasr i≥0andℓ i̸= 0and responses are observed exactly,
probes of all sizes identify the signed capacity measure˜µ= ∑ici sgn(ℓi)δ (ri,|ℓi|), and
nothing finer, for the hard limit and fortanh. Fortanh, probes in any neighbourhood
of zero suffice. For a cubic shape the result fails.
Parts (i)–(iii) are Supplement Theorems S6.1, S6.2 and S6.4; for the hard limit and
for tanh with probes of all sizes, part (iii) is machine-checked for any number of traders;
the small-probe statement is proved on paper. The theorem assumes exactly observed re-
sponses; with observation noise, a minimum-distance estimator recovers the signed measure
consistently (Supplement Proposition S12.6), and at the parametric rate for smooth shapes
and, with probes that bracket the kinks, for the hard limit (Companion Propositions C3.3
and C3.4); information about the order-nleverage moment grows like the probe size to the
power 2(2n+ 1) (Companion Proposition C3.2). Its last step, for tanh, is the uniqueness of
one-hidden-layer networks up to sign flips (Sussmann, 1992), which is why only the signed
measure is identified. What the market adds is the reduction: probing recovers the flow map
from prices, and two price dates give different memory–leverage pairs different scales.
The economic content is in part (ii). How close traders are to their limits, which governs
behaviour in a crash, leaves no trace in calm prices. It can be learned only by pushing
traders toward their limits or from disclosure. It also decides whether a breach cascades:
with mechanical impact, liquidation rounds settle smoothly when the liquidation triggered
per unit of price move,κℓ, is below one everywhere, and jump discontinuously where it
exceeds one (Companion Theorem C1.1). Calm prices therefore reveal neither the cascade
threshold nor which side of it the market is on. When dealers anticipate the liquidation
but capital arrives slowly, the price falls at once, overshoots, and the threshold is lower
(Companion Proposition C1.3).
23

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
9 Scope and conclusion
Evidence.The contribution is theoretical. Supplement Section S7 reports the registered
Taiwan and Binance tests with their readouts and proxy limitations. The original P1F
forecast window was void; the replacement window begins after this revision date and supplies
no result here. A separate account-record study is registered, but this package supplies no
completed collection or outcome. The local validation report separates recomputed synthetic
checks, formal proof checks, inherited empirical readouts and pending studies.
The sufficiency results hold whenever aggregate feedback flow is a causal function of public
signals; the kernel is the sufficient object when that function is linear, andνwhen it saturates
as in Section 8. The explicit identification results and the matching upper bounds need
linear rules with exponential memory; the lower bound on the cost of composition holds for
smooth families under the common-initialization and uniform derivative-moment conditions
(Theorem 4.5), and its attainment needs a strong-identifiability condition (Proposition 4.6).
For general rules, prices identify the aggregate flow map and the lower bounds still hold
(Supplement Theorem S6.7), and for rules within a bounded distance of linear, such as
dead bands, large probes identify composition (Companion Proposition C3.1); for saturating
families, probes that push traders toward their limits identify the signed capacity measure at
the parametric rate, and composition costs the linear amount when groups share a leverage
but an amount independent of their memory separation when leverages differ (Companion
Proposition C3.5); for some nonlinear families composition is not identified at all. For
linear rules with general completely monotone memory the minimax accuracy is of order
1/log(N/σ 2) (Supplement Theorem S18.2). Simulations of saturating, adaptive and neural
trading rules show where the linear theory holds and where it fails (Companion Section C4).
The passive bounds are stated for Gaussian news, the least favourable case (Supplement
Proposition S16.2); news with bounded support is not covered. Traders who learn from public
data, their own trades and private randomness are covered for what prices reveal: prices
identify their aggregate flow kernel and nothing finer, and linear learners whose parameters
drift are tracked below the speed limit of Section 7 (Companion Proposition C3.6). A
group that chooses its rule against an insider becomes a momentum trader that rides on her
information, and its choice is still summarized by the kernel (Companion Proposition C2.2).
Implications and limits.A uniform scaling of fixed positive feedback activity can be
evaluated from the aggregate budget. A restriction on one class can require information that
an otherwise informative public record does not supply precisely. This is a statement about
the specified interventions and losses; it does not make every market-wide policy identifiable
24

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
or every targeted policy infeasible. Class identifiers must correspond to executable accounts,
reports have noise and costs, and strategic responses can alter the counterfactual. The
companion work studies related safety decisions. A direct empirical test would measure
an externally documented class-specific intervention and evaluate predictions fixed before it
occurs. The present public-data forecast comparisons do not supply that causal test.

In thepositive economy(q i≥0),Jis entrywise nonnegative, its price row sums toS, and the

Lemma S1.1(Two motivating examples: filtering and learning).(i)Kalman memory.A desk
that tracks a latent random-walk trendvt+1 =v t+ζt+1,ζ∼N(0,σ 2ζ), from signalsr t =v t+νt,
ν∼N(0,σ 2ν), using the steady-state Kalman filter, updates asˆv t+1 = (1−η)ˆvt +ηr t with
η=
−σ2ζ +√σ4ζ + 4σ2ζσ2ν
2σ2ν ∈(0,1).(8)
(ii)Gradient memory.Online gradient descent withL 2 regularizationγ∈(0,1), step sizeα>0
and loss 12H(xt−θt)2 givesθ t+1 = (1−η)θ t +ηqx t withη=γ+αH∈(0,1)andq=αH/η.
A tabular value update with learning rateηat a fixed state has the same form; general
reinforcement-learning policies need not.
Why exponential memory, and what is assumed.Exponential memory is the old-
est model of expectations in economics: adaptive expectations (Cagan, 1956; Nerlove, 1958) and
geometric distributed lags (Koyck, 1954), and Muth (1960) showed that exponentially weighted
forecasts are optimal for a random walk observed with noise, which is Lemma S1.1(i). Lemma S1.1
shows that two different design philosophies, Bayesian filtering and regularized online learning,
produce the same reduced-form state. A third example is a desk that each period trades the
amount that maximizes ˆvtu− 12(κ+λ)u 2, a one-period trade-off between its trend forecast ˆvt and
quadratic impact and holding costs. Its trade isu t = ˆvt/(κ+λ). If the forecast is updated expo-
nentially, ˆvt+1 = (1−η)ˆvt +ηxt, the trade follows the same recursion,u t+1 = (1−η)u t +wxt with
w=η/(κ+λ). This is a static first-order condition, not a dynamic optimum. In these families the
memory rate is a known function of a tuning parameter, the desk’s signal-to-noise ratio, its regu-
larization or its cost parameters. That is the private information a supervisor would like to recover,
although real algorithms need not belong to any of these families. Applied to the pricing error, a
desk that cannot separate fundamental news from transitory deviations treats recent deviations as
a persistent trend, which is the exponential extrapolation of Barberis et al. (2015, 2018). Nothing
in the model says such trading is profitable.
The results use six modelling choices: linearity; price-first timing within a period; exponential
memory (needed for the identification result, Theorem S2.3, and the cost results of Section S4,
but not for sufficiency); algorithms that do not change their rules when observed (Proposition S5.7
relaxes this for one group); known primitives (a,κ); and stated observation contracts. Unknown
primitives can only enlarge the set of candidate populations, so the lower bounds remain valid;
Corollary S8.2 in the supplement shows that estimating them leaves the upper-bound rate un-
changed. Section S3 replaces the primitive (a,κ) by an equilibrium.
The observer.The cost results are stated for one observer outside the trader groups: a super-
visor, an exchange’s surveillance unit or a large desk. It knows (a,κ), the law of the observation
noise and, for theK-group results,K. It canwait, recording prices driven by natural innovations;
probe, trading a sequencev t against the groups from a reset state and recording the response; or
acquire disclosure, receiving each class’s flow. Independent resets are a benchmark that no outside
participant can run in a live market. We use them because they make the information content
of the data exact. Reset probing is a benchmark for what can be learned, not a proposed policy.
Section S4 shows separately that passive observation is no cheaper (Theorem S4.4).

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
S2 The kernel is a sufficient aggregate of the popula-
tion
This section states the paper’s main result. In a market with an insider, noise traders, a dealer and
feedback traders, all following arbitrary causal rules, the feedback traders affect the public paths,
insider profits and dealer inventories specified below only through their kernel. The notation for
the insider follows Kyle (1985), so in this sectionx t is the insider’s order, not the pricing error.
Theorem S2.1(The kernel is sufficient).Consider a market with the following participants and
rules.
•A valuevand noise tradesz t.
•An insider who submitsx t =X t(v,Ht−1).
•A dealer who setsp t =P t(Ht−1,yt)from the public historyH t−1 and the total order flow
yt =x t +zt +ft.
•Feedback groups whose memory states update on a public price statistics t =S t(p0,...,p t)
bym i,t+1 =r imi,t +s t fromm i,0 = 0, withr i = 1−η i and aggregate flowf t = ∑iwimi,t,
wi =q iηi.
The rulesX,PandSare arbitrary causal maps. This covers Kyle, inventory-averse, learning and
misspecified dealers, and any insider strategy.
(i)f t =∑m<tkmst−1−m, withk m =∑iwirmi , the moments of the merged measureM.
(ii) Two group configurations produce the same aggregate flow on datest≤Tfor every signal
sequence(s t)if and only ifk m =k′m for allm < T. On an unbounded horizon this holds if
and only if their merged measures coincide. For a fixed signal ruleS, the ‘if’ direction holds
for every price path. The ‘only if’ direction needs a rule that can produce an impulse signal.
It fails, for example, forS≡0, under which every configuration has zero flow.
(iii) Ifk m =k ′m form < T, then for every strategy profile and every realization of(v,z)the
following coincide ont≤T: the price and order-flow paths, the insider’s trades and realized
profit, and the dealer’s inventory. The two games therefore have the same strategy sets and
the same payoff for every profile. They have the same equilibria, with the same outcomes,
under any equilibrium concept defined from these payoffs and the public history.
(iv) Group-level quantities are not invariant. A Gauss and a Radau two-point configuration agree
onk 0,k 1,k 2, so they are outcome-equivalent forT= 3, while their largest groups carry weights
1
2 and 34.
Beyond exponential memory.The proof of part (iii) uses only one property of the feedback
traders: their aggregate flow is a fixed linear function of past public signals. So the result does not
depend on exponential memory.

any finite population of feedback tradersj= 1,...,Jwhose flows are causal linear functions of the
public signal,

(i) Two populations produce the same aggregate flow on datest≤Tfor every signal sequence if
and only if their kernels agree form<T.
(ii) If their kernels agree form < T, then for every strategy profile and every realization the
price and order-flow paths, the insider’s trades and realized profit, and the dealer’s inventory
coincide ont≤T, and the two games have the same equilibria under any equilibrium concept
defined from payoffs and the public history.
(iii) Any decomposition of a kernel into individual kernels is consistent with the same outcomes.
Prices, flows and all equilibrium outcomes therefore carry no information about the decom-
position beyondk.
Proof.(i) Aggregate flow isf t =∑m<tkmst−1−m. If the kernels agree form<T, the flows agree
ont≤Tfor every signal. Conversely, the impulse signals j =1{j= 0}givesf m+1 =k m. (ii)
The strong induction in the proof of Theorem S2.1(iii) (Supplement S9) uses only thatf t is the
same function of the signals beforetin both markets, which is part (i). (iii) follows from (ii), since
outcomes depend on the population only throughk.
S2.1 What prices identify
Theorem S2.1 says that populations with the same kernel cannot be told apart by anything that
price-based trading determines. The converse is that prices determine the kernel. In the probing
experiment of Section S1, the response of prices to trades is a fixed function of (a,κ,k), and it
determines the merged measure.
Theorem S2.3(Merged rate–weight equivalence).Letκ >0anda∈[0,1)be known. Use zero
initial states and a common probe contract, and observe the modeled price statex t.
(i)Equivalence class.Two economiesE A ={(q Ai ,ηAi )}andE B ={(q Bj ,ηBj )}produce identical
price responses for every probe sequence if and only if their merged measures coincide:
∑

Writea′ =a+κM({0}), so that groups withη= 1(rate0) act as part of the anchor. For
a′̸= 0the minimal realization has dimension1+|supp(M)∩(0,1)|, counting distinct positive
rates with nonzero merged weight after signed cancellation; fora ′ = 0the price carries no
state of its own and the dimension is|supp(M)∩(0,1)|. A group withη= 1merges into the
anchorawithout adding a pole.
(ii)Separation complexity under average power.Consider testing a single group with rate¯η
against two groups with rates¯η±δand the same total weight, on a compact family that is
uniformly Schur stable with all rates in[ϵ,1−ϵ]for someϵ >0. Observe˜x t =x t +νt with

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
The memory rates are poles ofGandzerosofH, not closed-loop poles. This has a practical
consequence verified in Niroula et al. (2026a): pole-fitting methods applied directly to the price
impulse response recover closed-loop eigenvalues (0.4256 and 0.8224 forK= 1,η= 0.3,a= 0.5,
κ= 0.8,q= 0.2) and miss the memory rate 0.70.
RemarkS2.4 (Scope).The result concerns the stated model and observation contract. Groups
sharing an exact rate enter prices through their merged weight; this does not determine identities
or wealth allocations. Nearby rates can be distinguishable with enough information. Theδ −4
statement in (ii) is a lower bound on a declared family, with family-dependent constants. A group
may choose its rate strategically. The observer then still sees only the merged measure of the rules
in use; Proposition S5.7 draws the consequence.
S3 Sufficiency and identification in equilibrium
The identification results of Section S2 take the price impactκand the persistenceaof pricing
errors as given. This section derives both from dealers who dislike holding inventory. It shows
that the equilibrium price is the closed loop of Section S1 with effective parameters and unchanged
memory rates. So the kernel stays the sufficient aggregate in equilibrium, and the cost of going
beyond it (Section S4) keeps its order.
An inventory equilibrium.
•Fundamental and price.The fundamentalF t is a public random walk with innovation variance
σ2F , and the price isp t =F t +xt.
•Groups.They tradeu i,t+1 =r iui,t +w ixt, the paper’s exponential memory withw i =q iηi
andr i = 1−η i.
•Exogenous flow.ξ t∼N(0,σ 2ξ), i.i.d., stands for noise trades or the observer’s probe. Cus-
tomer flow isy t =∑iui,t +ξt.
•Dealers.Competitive dealers absorb the flow and lay off a share 1−¯aof their inventory each
period, soH t = ¯aHt−1−yt. Each overlapping generation of dealers has CARA risk aversion
Aand holdsH t to the next date:H t = (Etpt+1−pt)/(AV), withV= Var t(pt+1).
We study stationary equilibria in whichx t is linear in the dealers’ inventory and the groups’ next
trades. WriteG(D) = ∑iwi/(D−r i) =∫ dM(r)/(D−r) for the Stieltjes transform of the merged
measure.

The identification results hold in equilibrium.With feedback, dealers price the groups’
predictable trades in advance, so the response of prices to flow is no longer the reduced form of
Section S1 with the primitive (a,κ). The next theorem shows that it is still that closed loop, exactly
and path by path, with new parameters and the same memory rates.
Theorem S3.2(The equilibrium is the paper’s closed loop).Take an equilibrium of Theorem S3.1
withD̸= ¯a, and set
a∗ = ¯aD, κ ∗ = κD,˜w i = D−¯aD−r iwi.
(i)Reduced form, pathwise.Let˜u i,t = D−¯aD−riui,t. For every realization, every initial state andeveryt,

So the price follows the closed loop(2)–(3)with persistencea ∗, impactκ ∗, exogenous flowξ
in place of the probe, and groups with the same memory rates and merged weights˜w i. Its
transfer function from flow to price is(5)with(a ∗,κ∗,˜w). The equilibrium is stable if and
only if this closed loop is.
(ii)Zeros.For¯a >0, distinct rates in(0,1)and nonzero weights, the memory rates are the
transmission zeros of the equilibrium price response, and none cancels (Theorem S4.3(i) with
the effective parameters).
(iii)Boundary.The effective merged measure is ˜M=hM, whereh(r) = (D−¯a)/(D−r)is
positive. The effective parameters determine the equilibrium ones:D= 1−κ ∗ ˜M([0,1])/(1−
a∗),¯a=a ∗Dandκ=κ ∗D. So the merged measure remains exactly what equilibrium prices
identify (Theorem S2.3).

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
(iv)The rates survive.Let(a ∗,κ∗,W,r c,∆η)satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem S4.1 with(a,κ) =
(a∗,κ∗). Then the twoK-group economies of that theorem are the price responses of two
inventory equilibria with identical primitives(A,σ 2F,σ 2ξ,¯a)and identical(κ,D):
•D= 1−κ ∗W/(1−a ∗), which exceeds every rate;
•¯a=a ∗Dandκ=κ ∗D;
•anyσ 2F,σ 2ξ >0, withA=κ ∗D(1−a ∗)/(σ2F +κ∗2σ2ξ);
•group weightsw i = ˜wi(D−r i)/(D−¯a)>0.
Hence an observer of equilibrium prices who knows the primitives needsN≥cσ 2(∆η)−(4K−2)
experiments. Theorem S4.2 applies to the effective parameters, and the true weights follow
from the effective ones by a Lipschitz map. SoN ∗≍σ 2(∆η)−(4K−2) in equilibrium.
In words.Dealers’ anticipation does not hide the groups and does not move them. It replaces the
persistence and impact by ¯a/Dandκ/D, and it rescales each group’s weight by (D−¯a)/(D−r i).
Because that factor rises withr i, slow-memory groups weigh more in equilibrium prices. The
memory rates are untouched.
•Every result of Sections S2 and S4 that concerns the map from order flow to prices therefore
holds in equilibrium: identification, sample complexity, passive observation, and probing
costs computed from the price path. Its constants are evaluated at the effective parameters.
•The lower-bound pair is not an artefact of the reduced form. Two economies with the same
dealers, the same risk aversion and the same impact differ only in their groups, and they are
as hard to tell apart as Theorem S4.1 says.
•Part (i), the realization in part (iv) and the effective group recursion are machine-checked
(Paper1EquilibriumReducedForm.lean).
•One assumption is new: the observer’s probe is part of the exogenous flow that dealers price
as noise. Dealers who recognized a probe would price it differently.
Further equilibrium results.Four results in Supplement S9 extend this section. None is
needed for the main argument.
•A strategic insiderwith short-lived information (Theorem S9.3). The equilibrium is char-
acterized in closed form and nests Kyle’s one-period values. Its coefficients depend on the
groups only throughG, and the observed price is the closed loop driven through a known
invertible moving-average filter. So the identification boundary and the (∆η) −(4K−2) rate
hold with an insider present. Positive feedback raises total impact, and the insider then
trades less and prices reveal less of her news.
•Fragility(Theorem S9.2). With positive feedback, a positive-impact equilibrium exists only
if dealer risk aversion lies below a threshold. For strong feedback the threshold is inversely
proportional to the feedback intensityG(1). With several groups the bound depends on the
population only throughG(1), again an aggregate.
•Rents(Theorem S9.4). A dealer that misjudges the aggregate response byεhands an insider
with a committed schedule a rent of exact orderε, or charges it one. Public data reveal the
aggregate response at the ordinary rate. So the information that protects price formation is
cheap to learn.
S-7

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
•A targeted halt in equilibrium(Theorem S9.5). Two markets that agree in equilibrium to
within the cube of the memory spacing can differ at order one after their largest group is
halted.
Disclosure keeps its value in equilibrium too (Corollary S9.1).
S4 The price of composition
Prices identify the kernel, and with exponential memory the kernel determines the merged measure.
A finite record, however, pins down only finitely many moments ofM, each with noise. This section
asks how much data it takes to recover the groups. The answer is the known rate for finite mixtures.
What the market adds is that the memories must first be extracted from a feedback loop, and that
waiting is no cheaper than trading.
S4.1 Many unknown groups
Theorem S4.1(K-group two-point lower bound).FixK≥2, a centerr c∈(0,1), total merged
weightW >0and0<∆η <min(r c,1−r c), so that all rates lie in(0,1), and assumea+
κW/(1−r c−∆η)<1, so that every candidate economy is Schur stable. Each reset experiment
observes the noisy tapsh 0,...,h T−1 withT≥2K+ 1(T=∞allowed), so that the first tap where
the two economies below differ,h 2K, is observed; the bound below uses the full response norm,
which dominates any finite prefix. There exist twoK-group economies with rates inr c + ∆η[−1,1],
positive weights, and merged measures whose moments of order0,...,2K−2coincide while the
moment of order2K−1differs by a constant times(∆η) 2K−1. Their impulse responses differ by
∥hA−h B∥2≍(∆η) 2K−1, so recovering the weights fromNindependent noisy reset experiments
with worst-case error probability below1/4requires
N≥cσ 2(∆η)−(4K−2).
The same requirement holds for the permutation-invariant lossW 1( ˆM,M)between estimated and
true merged measures at accuracyc KW∆η, where2c K >0is the Wasserstein distance between the
two normalized quadrature rules, so no relabelling of atoms escapes it.
The two losses have different targets. The weights of the two economies differ by a constant
that does not shrink with ∆η, so the first statement is about weights at fixed accuracy, which
Theorem S4.2 matches; the Wasserstein statement is about the memory distribution at accuracy
proportional to ∆η, which Niroula et al. (2026a) match with an upper bound.
The construction uses theK-point Gauss and Gauss–Radau rules of the same weight function:
both are positive, haveKatoms and integrate polynomials of degree 2K−2 exactly. ForK= 2
it reproduces the order of Theorem S11.1. Numerically, their low moments agree to machine
precision and their moment of order 2K−1 differs by 0.222, 0.0533 and 0.0131 forK= 2,3,4.
The gap is known exactly: it isg K = 4K/(K(2KK)2)≈π4 −K (Supplement Proposition S17.1).
It enters the divergence squared, so the data requirement carries a factorg −2K ≈16 K/π2 on top
of the exponent; this is one reason the constants grow fast withK. The two rules also differ
in their largest weight for everyKwe checked: forK= 2,...,200 the largest Gauss–Radau
weight exceeds the largest Gauss weight, by 0.25 atK= 2 and by about 0.79/K 2 for largeK
(equilibrium/gauss_radau_maxweight.py). So the argument given forK= 2 in Proposition S5.1
applies to each suchK. The comparison holds for everyK≥2, with differenceπ/(4K 2) +O(K−3)
S-8

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
(Supplement Proposition S17.2). The supervisory result needs less: Lemma S12.3 proves that, for
everyK, some two rules of this kind have different largest weights.
What counts as an experiment.In Theorem S4.1 an experiment is a unit impulse from
the reset state whose response is observed with noise of varianceσ 2 per tap. The exponent is
the same for any probe design of bounded energy. For any probevthe mean difference between
the two economies is at most sup ω|∆ ˆH(eiω)|∥v∥ 2, and supω|∆ ˆH|=O((∆η) 2K−1) because ∆ ˆH=
z−1 ˆHA ˆHB ∆Gwith both responses bounded on the circle. Experiments with probe energyE
each therefore needNE≳σ 2(∆η)−(4K−2): a larger probe buys the same information with fewer
experiments, and the account-level analysis of Niroula et al. (2026a) prices the capital that a larger
probe requires. The bound does not cover information obtained without trading against the groups,
such as class records (Section S5).
Theorem S4.2(Matching upper bound for everyK).FixK≥2, a compact setS⊂{s∈[−1,1] K :
s1 <···< s K}of ordered rescaled rates withmin i̸=j|si−s j|≥γ >0, and merged weightsw i∈

Smaller classes and constants.On smaller classes the upper bound need not be tight: if the
rates are known, for example, two weights need only orderσ 2(∆η)−2 experiments. ForKknown
clustered rates the requirement is at least of orderσ 2(∆η)−2(K−1). A weight perturbation along the

of orderK−1 by order (∆η) K−1. ForK= 3, the direction (1,−2,1) on ratesr c−∆η,r c,rc + ∆η
changes the second moment by 2(∆η)2. This is still far below the unknown-rate order (∆η)−(4K−2).
The constantsc 0 andCgrow quickly withK, through the conditioning of the moment map; the
theorem gives the order in ∆ηfor fixedK, not a usable constant for largeK(in Niroula et al.
(2026a) our double-precision Prony implementation already fails atK= 4 on the disclosure grid).
S4.2 Passive data
Theorem S4.3(Passive identification: minimum phase).Consider a stable economy with0<a<
1, distinct memory rates0<r i<1and nonzero merged weights.
(i) The zeros ofHare the memory ratesr i together with a structural zero atz= 0; none cancels
against the denominator, and all lie in the open unit disk, soHis minimum phase. The
degenerate anchor (a= 0) and zero-rate cases require the cancellations described in the proof
of Theorem S2.3.
S-9

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
(ii) Let the price be driven by a single innovation sequence with varianceσ 2ξ >0, possibly un-
known, so that its spectrum isf(ω) =σ 2ξ|H(eiω)|2/(2π). Within the class of (i),fdetermines
σ2ξκ2 and the normalized responseH/h 0 (recallh 0 =κ); hence it determines the memory
rates. If in additionκ(orσ 2ξ ) andaare known, it determinesHand, by Theorem S2.3, the
merged measure.
The result holds for signed weights (mean-reverting groups): in 1,733 stable random economies
with weights of both signs, the nonstructural zeros equalled the memory rates and lay inside the
unit disk. With a second independent shock in the groups’ memories, the Jacobian of the price
spectrum with respect to six model parameters has full rank, with singular values from 34.51 down
to 0.0698. Passive noise helps identification rather than hindering it.
Theorem S4.4(Passive cost).Suppose the price is observed passively as the stationary output
xt =H θ(L)ξt of i.i.d. Gaussian innovationsξ t∼N(0,σ 2),σ 2 >0known, without observation
noise, forθin a compact, uniformly Schur-stable family with rates in[η min,η max]⊂(0,1), and let
D(θ1∥θ0)be the Kullback–Leibler divergence rate per observation.
(i) For a single group at¯ηagainst the symmetric pair at¯η±δwith the same total weight,
D= Θ(δ 4).
(ii) For the moment-matched two-group economies of Theorem S11.1,D= Θ(τ 2(∆η)6).
(iii) For everyK≥2, for theK-group Gauss and Gauss–Radau economies of Theorem S4.1,
D= Θ((∆η) 4K−2).
Hence any test based onTpassive observations with worst-case error at mostα <1/4needs
T= Ω(δ −4),T= Ω(τ −2(∆η)−6)andT= Ω((∆η) −(4K−2))respectively, uniformly as the separation
shrinks: passive data cost at least the same polynomial order as active probing for every number
of groups. For each fixed pair, at fixed type-I error the likelihood-ratio test’s type-II error decays
at exponential rateD(Stein’s lemma), and both errors decay at the Chernoff-information rate,
which is of the same order in the separation. So the orders are the right ones. We do not claim
that this attainment is uniform as the separation shrinks. All constants depend only onK, the
stability margin of the family, the rate interval[η min,η max], the total weight and the moment-
matched construction; they do not depend onδ,∆ηorT. In part (ii)τis held fixed as∆η→0.
The testing conclusions use a finite-record boundDKL(PT1∥PT0 )≤CTε 2, withεthe leading moment
gap (δ2,τ(∆η) 3,(∆η) 2K−1); the lower bounds onDand the fixed-pair error exponents are separate
statements.
Near instability.Theorem S4.4 is stated on a family that stays uniformly stable. One might
hope that the barrier lifts as the market approaches instability, since the loop then amplifies every
difference. It does not. What becomes visible is the distance to instability, not the composition.
Proposition S4.5(The barrier does not lift near instability).FixK≥2and theK-group Gauss
and Gauss–Radau economiesAandBof Theorem S4.1, with positive weights, rates in(0,1)and
κ >0. For a marginϵ >0, choose persistencesa A(ϵ)anda B(ϵ)so that both economies have
feedback budgetS= 1−ϵ, and supposea A(ϵ),aB(ϵ)>0forϵ≤ϵ 0. LetD ϵ(B∥A)be the Kullback–
Leibler divergence rate between the stationary price processes driven by i.i.d.N(0,σ 2)innovations.
There isC, not depending onϵor∆η, such that
Dϵ(B∥A)≤C(∆η) 4K−2 for all0<ϵ≤ϵ 0 and all small∆η.

Figure S1: The passive barrier near instability. Both panels show the Gaussian Kullback–
Leibler information per observation between the stationary price processes of two populations
whose merged measures are theK-node Gauss and Gauss–Radau rules on memories spread
over ∆ηaround 0.5 (total weight 0.3, impactκ= 0.5). (a) At a common feedback budget
one millionth below instability, the information falls like (∆η) 4K−2 (dotted lines). (b) Held
at a common budget (solid), the information stays bounded as the margin 1−Sshrinks;
held at a common persistence (dashed), it grows without bound, because the budgets then
differ. Computed from the closed-form spectra; scripts inresearch_extension/fragility_
reveals/.
In words.Even as the market approaches a crash, passive prices reveal no more about who
is trading than they did in calm conditions, per observation. They reveal more and more about
how close the market is to instability: if the two economies share the persistenceainstead of
the budget, their budgets differ by order (∆η) 2K−1, and numerically the divergence rate then
grows likeϵ −1. The proof is in Supplement S12.2. Two limits apply. The statement is about
the divergence rate per observation; near instability prices are strongly dependent, and a finite-
sample bound that is uniform inϵis not proved, although numerically the exact information in
nobservations stays within 3% belowntimes the rate down toϵ= 10 −3 (Supplement S12.2).
And probing does gain near instability: the squared response difference grows likeϵ −1 (numerical,
research_extension/fragility_reveals), but probing a market at the edge of instability is not
a policy. Figure S1 shows both statements.
S5 Who needs composition, and what disclosure buys
By Theorem S2.1, composition is worth nothing to anyone trading on public information. It can
still matter to a supervisor whose action is aimed at one group. This section gives a decision in
which it does and the disclosure that removes the cost of learning it. It ends with a bound on what
price-based targeting can achieve when a group can hide.
A decision in which attribution matters.A supervisor must keep the feedback budget
S=a+κ ∑iqi below a prudential ceiling ¯s. It can either cut all activity uniformly or halt the
S-11

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
largest verified class. The target is the feedback budget, not the spectral radius of the state matrix:
in an already stable positive economy, imposingS≤¯s<1 is a tighter prudential constraint, not a
restoration of stability.
Proposition S5.1(Supervisory separation: market-wide versus targeted intervention).Assume
the positive stable model, knowna,κ, anda<¯s<1. Writeq i =w i/(1−r i)andR=S−κmax iqi
for the post-halt feedback budget.
(i) A uniform scaleθmeets the ceiling exactly whena+θ(S−a)≤¯s. Its largest feasible value
isθ ∗ = min{1,(¯s−a)/(S−a)}. The identityS= 1−κ/ ∑jhj is algebraic; estimating
this infinite sum requires a justified tail bound and an observation/noise model. It alone
establishes no unconditional parametric learning rate.
(ii) ForK= 2, the Gauss and Gauss–Radau pair of Theorem S4.1 has limiting largest normalized
weights1/2and3/4. Thus|S G−S R|=O((∆η) 3)andq Rmax−q Gmax =W/(4(1−r c)) +
O(∆η). For sufficiently small spacing, a nonempty interval of ceilings separates their post-
halt decisions. ForNindependent reset records with common Gaussian noiseσ 2I, both
decision errors at most1/4require
N≥(2Φ −1(0.75)σ/∥hG−hR∥2)2≥cσ 2(∆η)−6.
The record horizon and the response norm must be the same on both sides.
(iii) Tagged-flow estimation under Theorem S5.3, a rate bound away from one, and a fixed nonzero
decision margin gives a bounded sample requirement as the spacing shrinks. The target class
must be mapped to an executable, verified class label; a largest latent atom alone supplies no
such identity.
The proof is in Supplement S12. Proposition S12.4 there extends part (ii) to everyK, with
exponent 4K−2.
S5.1 What class-tagged disclosure buys
Proposition S5.2(Tagged flow removes the barrier).Let the pricex t be the causal stationary
solution of the model driven by i.i.d. centred innovations of varianceσ 2ξ ∈(0,∞)in a positive,
stable economy, with stationary autocovariancesγ x(s) = ∑ℓcℓλ|s|ℓ (cℓ≥0,0≤λ ℓ <1the closed-
loop modes). Suppose groupihas merged weightw i >0and the observer receives prices and
each group’s flow˜u i,t =u i,t +ε i,t,u i,t =q iyi,t, with i.i.d. centred measurement noise of finite

problem. Proposition S5.2 says that tagging removes the barrier; the next result says by how much.

Proposition S5.2 and Theorem S5.3 use different observation arrangements: the proposition
concerns passive, stationary data with prices observed without noise, where the price is a valid
instrument; the theorem concerns reset experiments with noisy prices. With noisy prices, regressing
flow on the observed price is an errors-in-variables problem, which is why the theorem works from
the known impulse instead.
S5.2 Partial disclosure
Actual regimes tag some traders and not others: Japan registers high-speed traders, India identifies
algorithmic orders, and everyone else stays anonymous. Theorem S5.3 prices full tagging. The next
result prices tagging some of the groups.

S5.3 Pooling markets
Algorithms trade in many markets at once. A trend follower’s lookback is a property of the
algorithm, the same in every futures contract it trades, while how much it trades in each market
varies. A supervisor who sees several markets therefore sees several kernels built from the same
memory rates with different weights. The next result says what that is worth.
TakeDmarkets, each with known persistence and impact and its own independent noise,
and the sameKalgorithms with memory ratesr i =r c + ∆ηs i in every market and merged
weightsW ij >0 in marketj. Every market is Schur stable for every configuration considered,
aj +κj
∑
iWij/(1−r c−∆η)<1. In each market the supervisor runsNreset experiments as in
Theorem S4.2. Letq ∗ =⌈K/D⌉andl ∗ =K−1 +q ∗.
Theorem S5.5(Pooling markets).(i)Lower bound.Near every configuration(s,W)with dis-
tinct nodes at which the moment map below orderl ∗ has locally constant rank (an open dense
set), there are two configurations at a fixed distance in(s,W), with distinct nodes and posi-
tive weights, whose kernels agree in every market up to the moments of orderl ∗−1. Telling
them apart with error probability below1/4needsN≥c(∆η) −2l∗.
(ii)Upper bound.At configurations where the rescaled moment map up to orderl ∗ has an injec-
tive differential (condition (R)), a minimum-distance estimator restricted to a neighbourhood
recovers the rescaled nodes and the weights with errorOP (N−1/2(∆η)−l∗), soN=C(∆η) −2l∗
experiments suffice locally.
(iii)The order is sharp.Condition (R) never holds below orderl ∗, and for everyKandD≤K
it holds at orderl ∗ outside a proper algebraic subset of configurations, so at almost every
configuration.
(iv)Globally.Take a compact class of configurations with nonempty interior, separated nodes and
weights bounded below. Letl c be the least order at which the moment map is injective on the
class up to relabelling, andl g≥l c the least order at which it is, in addition, an immersion
on the class. Over that class the data needed are at least of order(∆η) −2lc and at most of
order(∆η)−2lg, withl ∗≤l c≤l g≤2K−1; if condition (R) holds at orderl ∗ everywhere on
the class,l g =l c and the order is exact. Pooling never needs more data than one market.
So the data requirement is of order(∆η) −2(K−1+⌈K/D⌉) :(∆η) −(4K−2) with one market, as in
Theorem S4.1, and(∆η) −2K withKor more markets. Markets whose weights are linear combi-
nations of other markets’ weights add data but do not lower the exponent;Dis the rank of the
participation matrixW.
Proof.Writeµ jl = ∑iWij(ri−r c)l = (∆η)ltjl witht jl = ∑iWijsli; the map from (s,W) to the
