is an immersion; on a neighbourhood its image is a manifold on which the map has a Lipschitz
inverse, and the minimum-distance estimator, which projects ˆtonto that image, inherits the error.
(iv)Lower bound.Ifl c > l∗, by the definition ofl c the class contains two configurations that are
not relabellings of each other and share all moments below orderl c; their distance is positive and
does not depend on ∆η, and the two-point argument of part (i) givesN≥c(∆η) −2lc. Ifl c =l∗,
part (i) applied at an interior point of constant rank gives the same order. Andl c≥l∗, because
by part (i) the moment map below orderl ∗ is not injective near such a point.Upper bound.An
injective immersion of a compact set is an embedding with a Lipschitz inverse on its image, so
the minimum-distance estimator over the class has errorO P (N−1/2(∆η)−lg). If (R) holds at order
l∗ on the whole class, it holds at every higher order, since rows are only added, so the map is
an immersion at orderl c andl g =l c. Andl g ≤2K−1 because one market’s moments up to
order 2K−1 determine itsKnodes and weights (Prony), with injective differential, after which
every other market’s weights solve a nonsingular Vandermonde system. (iii) Below orderl ∗ there
are fewer equations than unknowns, so the differential cannot be injective. Failure of (R) is the
vanishing of every maximal minor of the Jacobian, a polynomial condition, so (R) holds outside
a proper algebraic set as soon as it holds at one configuration, which need not lie in the class.
For one configuration, letg(i) =imodDand give marketjweight one on the algorithms with
g(i) =jand zero on the others, with distinct nodes. Each group has at most⌈K/D⌉members.
The derivative with respect to nodes i is nonzero only in the rows of marketg(i), so the Jacobian is
block-diagonal by market, and the block of marketjhas thel ∗ + 1 =K+⌈K/D⌉rowsl= 0,...,l ∗
and the columns (sli)l for everyiand (ls l−1i )l foriin the group. If a combination of these columns
vanishes, the functionalp↦→ ∑iaip(si) +∑i∈Gjbip′(si) kills every polynomial of degree at most
l∗. Testing it on (X−s i)∏k̸=i(X−s k)mk and on ∏k̸=i(X−s k)mk, withm k = 2 in the group
and 1 outside, whose degrees are belowK+|G j|≤l ∗ + 1, givesb i = 0 and thena i = 0. So each
block, and the Jacobian, is injective. An exact computation (rank modulo a large prime at random
integer configurations) agrees for everyK≤8,D≤K.
Proposition S5.6(A global identification certificate for pooled moments).SupposeKis known
and the normalized moments aret d,m =∑Ki=1Wdismi , with distinct ordered real nodes and strictly
positive weights, observed ford= 1,...,Dandm= 0,...,L,L≥K. Form theD(L−K+ 1)×K
block-Hankel matrix and vector
H(d,j),ℓ =t d,j+ℓ, b (d,j) =−t d,j+K,0≤j≤L−K,0≤ℓ<K.
S-16

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
IfrankH=K, the moments identify all nodes and weights globally, up to relabelling, among
populations withKdistinct nodes. AtL=l ∗ =K−1 +⌈K/D⌉, this condition holds outside
a proper algebraic subset of the parameter space. On any compact ordered class with separated
nodes, bounded weights, andrankH=Kthroughout, the moment map has a Lipschitz inverse and
injective differential. On a class with nonempty interior, the global observation-cost exponent in
Theorem S5.5 is therefore2l ∗.
Proof.LetP(z) = ∏i(z−si) =z K+∑K−1ℓ=0 cℓzℓ. MultiplyingP(s i) = 0 byWdisji and summing gives
Hc=b. Full column rank makescunique. Any otherK-node population with the same moments
has a monic annihilating polynomial satisfying the same system, so it has the same polynomial
and the same roots. The firstKmoments in each market then determine its weights through the
nonsingular Vandermonde matrix. This is a uniqueness argument, without an optimization search.
For genericity letq=⌈K/D⌉andL=K−1 +q. WithV i,ℓ =s ℓi, one hasH=BV, where
B(d,j),i =W disji, 0≤j <q. Assign each node to one ofDgroups of size at mostq, give it weight
one in its assigned market and zero elsewhere, and take distinct nodes. The columns ofBthen form
disjoint Vandermonde blocks, each of full column rank. ThusHhas rankK. A sufficiently small
positive perturbation of every zero weight preserves a nonzero minor and gives strictly positive
weights. The relevant minors are polynomials, so rank failure is a proper algebraic condition. This
proves genericity; it does not assert full rank at every positive configuration.
For stability of the inverse,c= (H ⊤H)−1H⊤bis smooth whereverHhas full column rank.
Roots ofPare locally smooth functions of its coefficients when they are simple, and each market’s
weights are smooth functions of the ordered roots and its firstKmoments. These maps give
a local smooth left inverse of the moment map, hence an injective differential. On the stated
compact class, the smallest singular value ofHand the node gaps are bounded away from zero.
Local inverse bounds, compactness and injectivity give a global Lipschitz inverse: otherwise pairs
with a vanishing image-to-parameter distance ratio would either have distinct limits, contradicting
injectivity, or a common limit, contradicting a local inverse bound. The upper rate follows by the
rescaled-moment argument of Theorem S5.5(ii); its lower rate holds on the class’s interior by part
(i).
This certificate is the multi-channel annihilating-polynomial method underlying Prony recon-
struction, not a new signal-processing principle. It supplies a direct condition for the financial
pooling model and closes the generic global-uniqueness question atl ∗. Rank-deficient exceptional
classes, unknown numbers of types, misspecified shared memories, cross-impact, and a robust nu-
merical rank decision under noise require further treatment. The smallest singular value matters:
full rank in exact arithmetic alone is not a guarantee of useful finite-sample precision. The proof
is on paper; exact rational tests exercise the recurrence and its reconstruction for 2≤K≤8 and
1≤D≤K.
What this means.Under the pooling assumption, an algorithm has a common memory across
markets. Variation in its participation can then improve identification. Pooling price records
from markets where the same algorithms trade in different proportions lowers the exponent of the
composition barrier, from 4K−2 to 2(K−1 +⌈K/D⌉), without any disclosure. Cross-market
surveillance is a partial substitute for tagging. The converse is a constraint on hiding: to stay as
hidden as in a single market, the algorithms must trade in the same proportions in every market, so
that the participation matrix has rank one. For two algorithms with half-lives between 5 and 9 days
(memory rates 0.9±0.025, total weight 0.06, impact 0.5, feedback budget 0.9, passive Gaussian
records), the Gauss and Gauss–Radau pair needs about 7,000 years of daily prices in one market.
S-17

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
In two markets in which the algorithms’ shares differ by 50 percentage points, the hardest pair
found by a numerical search needs about 90 years; with shares differing by 20 points, about 4,000.
At half the memory spacing the one-market figure rises to 400,000 years and the two-market figures
to about 1,300 and 55,000. The gain grows like (∆η) −2 as memories approach each other, and it
depends on how differently the algorithms allocate across markets. These two-market figures come
from a numerical search for the hardest pair under stated constraints; the true worst case can only
be harder.
Relation to the literature, and checks.The mathematics is the multi-snapshot version
of super-resolution, where several observations of the same nodes with different amplitudes are
known to improve resolution (Li et al., 2022); the contribution here is the explicit market obser-
vation contract, its local and rank-certified global cost, and its intervention interpretation. We
do not claim the underlying multi-channel Prony method as new. The exponent is confirmed by
the smallest singular value of the Jacobian in 60-digit arithmetic (local slopes 3,2; 5,4,3; 7,5,5,4
forK= 2,3,4) and by exact rank computations (research_extension/cross_market/, which
also holds the calibration scripts). The counting steps are machine-checked (p1 pool rows lt,
p1 pool rows ge,p1 pool single market,p1 pool many marketsinPaper1PoolingMarkets.
lean). Proposition S5.6 supplies a block-Hankel rank certificate for global recovery atl ∗ and
proves that it holds generically. A compact class entirely satisfying that certificate hasl c =l g =l∗.
Numerical multistart searches are supplementary diagnostics: approximate roots near colliding
nodes do not certify distinct exact populations, and absence of a found alternative is not a proof.
The fresh extended-search output and its residual checks are included in the revision audit. Monte
Carlo runs of the local estimator of part (ii), with the search started at the true configuration, con-
firm the attained rates: the weight error grows like (∆η) −l∗ with fitted slopes−3.02,−1.98,−4.92
and−2.95 for (K,D) = (2,1),(2,2),(3,1),(3,3), against−3,−2,−5,−3 (research_extension/
theorem_checks/mc_rates.py). The lower bound on joint records of all markets is machine-
checked for any matched pair (p1 pool lower bound matched,Paper1PoolingLower.lean), with
an explicit pair for two algorithms in two markets (p1 pool explicit pair K2D2). The upper
bounds are machine-checked under condition (R): the local minimum-distance estimator, and on
a compact class on which the moment map is injective the global one, is within (2/c) 2 le(∆η)−l
of the truth when every kernel tap up to orderlis withine; at order 2K−1 the moment map
is injective on ordered configurations with nonzero weights in one market, with injective deriva-
tive, sol g ≤2K−1 (p1 pool upper local,p1 pool upper global,p1 pool injective top,
p1 pool fderiv injective top,Paper1PoolingUpper.lean). Not covered: markets with cross-
impact, and algorithms whose memory differs across markets.
S5.4 Traders who respond to being watched
The results so far take the groups’ rules as given. A group that expects a targeted action has
a reason to change its rule. Sufficiency bounds what a supervisor who relies on prices can then
achieve.
Proposition S5.7(Hiding caps targeting).A supervisor observes prices, under any probes and
for a record of any length, and then targets a given group or not. Being targeted costs the group
P >0. The group chooses among available rules. Ruleρhas a private costC(ρ)≥0and leads
to targeting with probabilityπ(ρ). Suppose one available ruleρ 0, with costC 0, gives the population
the kernel of a population that the supervisor targets with probabilityα.
(i)π(ρ 0) =α, whatever the supervisor’s probes, record length and decision rule.
S-18

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
(ii) Every ruleρ ∗ that minimizes the group’s expected costC(ρ) +Pπ(ρ)satisfies
π(ρ∗)≤α+ C0−C(ρ∗)P ≤α+ C0P .
(iii) Suppose the group has two rules: its original rule, with cost zero and targeting probabilityπ T
on a record of lengthT, andρ 0. It adoptsρ 0 wheneverπ T > α+C 0/P. Ifπ T →1and
P(1−α)>C 0, this holds for all largeT, and the targeting probability is thenα.
Proof.(i) By Theorem S2.1, populations with the same kernel give the same law of the price record
for every probe sequence and every insider and dealer rule. The supervisor’s decision is a function
of that record and of its own randomization. (ii)C(ρ ∗) +Pπ(ρ ∗)≤C 0 +Pαby optimality, and
C(ρ∗)≥0. (iii) The original rule costsPπ T andρ 0 costsC 0 +Pα.
S6 Beyond the kernel: saturating traders and stress
The kernel is a linear object, and every precedent for it is linear. Real feedback traders have
limits: position caps, margin, risk budgets. This section asks what replaces the kernel when trades
saturate, and what prices reveal about the part that saturation adds. The answer is a sharper
version of the paper’s main message. In calm markets, prices reveal the kernel and nothing else.
How close traders are to their limits, which is what governs behaviour under stress, is invisible in
calm data. It can be learned only by pushing traders toward their limits, or from disclosure.
Saturating traders.In the market of Theorem S2.1, let traderihave memory rater i∈[0,1),

and at the band edge its position is capped atc i. Inside the bands the population is the linear one
of Section S1, with merged weightsw i =c iℓi and kernelk m =∑iciℓirmi . Write

S6.1 What stress reveals
Theorem S6.2 says what calm data cannot show. The next result says what stress does show:
everything that matters. Write

for oddψ.
The result holds for any shape with one property. An odd shapeψhasindependent dilationsif
rescaled copies of it at distinct scales are linearly independent: for any distincts 1,...,s n >0 and
any weightsw 1,...,w n,∑kwkψ(sku) = 0 for everyuimpliesw 1 =···=w n = 0.
Theorem S6.4(Stress identifies the leverage distribution).Letψbe odd with independent dilations,
and let every trader haver i≥0andℓ i̸= 0. In the probing experiment of Section S1, with reset
state and exactly observed responses, two populations give identical price responses to every probe
sequence if and only if˜µ P = ˜µP′. Probe responses therefore identify, within each memory class,
the net capacity at every leverage level. The kernelk m =
∫
λrm ˜µ(dr,dλ)is one linear functional
of this measure.
The property holds or fails as follows.
(i) The hard limit has independent dilations. For it the result needs probes of all sizes: probes
that keep every memory state inside every band identify only the kernel (Theorem S6.2(iv)).
(ii)tanhhas independent dilations. Moreover, identical responses to all probes in an arbitrarily
small neighbourhood of zero already imply˜µP = ˜µP′.
(iii) A cubic shapeψ(u) =u−γu 3 does not: the scales1,2,3with weights5,−4,1give∑
kwkψ(sku) = 0for everyu. For such a shape, probes identify only the first and third
leverage moments within each memory.
Machine check.The general statement of Theorem S6.4 is machine-checked for any
number of traders (p1 thm stress identificationinPaper1StressIdentificationGeneral.
lean). So are the first sentence of part (i) (p1 clip indep dilations), the first
sentence of part (ii) (p1 tanh indep dilations) and the counterexample in part (iii)
(p1 cubic not indep dilations). The theorem for the two shapes, with no hypothesis left on
ψ, isp1_thm_stress_identification_hard_limitandp1_thm_stress_identification_tanh.
Two things are proved on paper only: the small-probe statement in part (ii), and the last sentence
of part (iii). In the formal statement, “identical responses” reads: every pair of a probe and a price
path of the first market is such a pair of the second.
The proof (Supplement S13) uses three facts. First, probes can steer the price along any path,
so identical responses mean identical maps from past prices to flow. Second, two dates of prices
turn that map into a sum of scaled copies ofψ, one per memory–leverage pair, with distinct scales
for a suitable direction. Third, independent dilations separate the copies. For the hard limit, each
copy adds a kink where its pair saturates, and the copy with the largest scale has the first kink. For
tanh, each copy approaches its limit at its own exponential rate, and the copy with the smallest
scale has the slowest tail. The kink argument is constructive. In the example of Figure S2, read
along one direction, the flow map has kinks at 3.702 and 14.813, and their slope jumps return net
capacities 0.24989 and 0.99988, against true values 0.25 and 1. Locating the second group needs
a memory state of almost 15, about four times the stress level at which the first trader saturates.
Knowing the kernel and the first group fixes only the second group’s totalcℓ, not its leverage.
The theorem assumes exactly observed responses; with observation noise, a minimum-distance
estimator recovers ˜µconsistently (Supplement Proposition S12.6), and for smooth shapes at the
parametric rate (Companion Proposition C3.3). Theorem S6.4 turns Section S6 into a two-layer
theory. Prices in calm markets identify the kernel. Prices under stress identify the full signed
measure ˜µ, which by Theorem S6.1 is all that any price-based outcome depends on. Under hard
limits, no information beyond the kernel arrives until the first group saturates. Under smooth limits,
the order-nterm of the response scales with the probe size to the power 2n+1. Its information scales
S-22

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
with the power 2(2n+ 1): Proposition S6.3 proves this forn= 1 and Companion Proposition C3.2
for everyn.
S6.2 Deleveraging traders and dealers who anticipate
Proposition S6.5(Deleveraging traders).Let each trader follow any causal rule, for example
deleveraging gradually, liquidating, or switching strategy after a loss, provided it trades linearly,
wimi,t, as long as its state has stayed inside its band on every date up tot. Then Theorem S6.2
holds verbatim for two such populations with the same kernel. Up to the first date at which some
trader leaves its band, the paths coincide and equal the linear market’s path, and parts (ii)–(iv)
follow.
The proof of the pathwise part is the induction of Theorem S6.2(i). It is machine-checked
for arbitrary causal rules outside the bands (p1 delever stress invariance). Deleveraging that
starts before a hard limit only moves the band edge inward. Calm data remain silent until the first
trader starts to cut its position.
After the first breach: a threshold.If traders who breach their limits liquidate, their sales
move the price, and the price move pushes other traders toward their own limits. With mechanical
impactκand a densityℓof positions per unit distance to their limits, Companion Theorem C1.1
shows that the liquidation rounds converge to the least fixed point ofδ=s+κL(δ). Ifκℓ <1
everywhere, the fall is unique and continuous in the shock. Ifκℓ >1 somewhere, there is a shock
level at which an arbitrarily small further shock produces a discrete further fall. Calm prices reveal
neitherℓnor which side of the threshold the market is on.
Proposition S6.6(Who sets prices matters).(i) Theorems S6.2 and S6.4 and Proposi-
tion S6.5 hold for any causal dealer rule that is the same in the two markets. This covers
every rule that depends on the population only through its kernel, for example a dealer who
knows only the kernel, or one who prices current flow.
(ii) A dealer who knows the population and quotes on the expected next-period feedback flow uses
more than the kernel. Suppose the next memory states are Gaussian,m +g ∼N( ¯mg,s 2g), with
every trader inside its band in expectation,|ℓ g ¯mg|<1, and hard limits. Then the expected
next-period flows under two populations with the same kernel differ by at most
∑
g∈P∪P′

√
2/π σge−δ2g/(2σ2g), σ g =|ℓ g|sg, δ g = 1−|ℓ g ¯mg|.
The proof is in Supplement S13.
S6.3 Any population
The results above describe two families of rules: linear rules, summarized by the kernel, and
saturating rules, summarized by the measureν. The next theorem drops the family. A population
is now represented only by its aggregate feedback flow mapF, which sends a price path to the
flow at each date and may be any causal function: linear, saturating, deleveraging, or switching
between strategies after observing past profits.
Theorem S6.7(Any population).In the probing experiment of Section S1, with reset state, exactly
observed responses andκ̸= 0, let two populations have aggregate flow mapsFandF ′.
S-23

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
(i)Sufficiency.IfF=F ′ on every price path, the two markets give the same prices, order flows,
insider profits and dealer inventories for every strategy profile and realization, and the same
equilibria.
(ii)Identification.If every pair of a probe sequence and a price path produced by the first popula-
tion is produced by the second, thenF=F ′ on every price path. So the two populations give
identical responses to every probe sequence if and only ifF=F ′, and a group-level quantity,
such as the weight of the largest group, is identified from probe responses if and only if it is
a function ofF.
(iii)Cost.LetCbe any class of populations that contains the two linearK-group economies of
Theorem S4.1(i). Then any procedure that decides a group-level quantity separating them,
overC, fromNreset impulse-response records needsN≥cσ 2(∆η)−(4K−2), and from passive
Gaussian records needs at least the number of observations of Theorem S4.4. For impulse-
response records, every band-linear class whose capacities can be large enough that the im-
pulse responses keep every trader inside its band contains populations with exactly the two
economies’ responses; for passive Gaussian records, the same holds up to an arbitrarily small
loss in the error probabilities if the capacities in the class are unbounded.
Proof.Part (i) is the induction of Theorem S2.1, which uses only that the flow is a fixed causal
function of past public signals. For part (ii), given any price pathx ∗, the probev t = (x∗t−
ax∗t−1)/κ−F(x ∗)t makes the first market followx ∗, because the flow at datetdepends only on
prices beforet. By hypothesis the second market also followsx ∗ under this probe, and subtracting
the two price equations givesF(x ∗)t =F ′(x∗)t at every date. The converse is part (i) applied
to the probing recursion. A quantitygis identified exactly whengagrees on populations with
identical responses, that is, on populations with the sameF. For part (iii), a procedure that works
overCworks in particular on the two economies, so the lower bounds of Theorems S4.1 and S4.4
apply. For a band-linear class, take capacitiesc i and leveragesℓ i =w i/ci withc i large enough
that|ℓ imi,t|≤1 along the impulse response, which is bounded; inside the bands each trader trades
ciℓimi,t =w imi,t, so the population reproduces the linear economy’s responses (Theorem S6.2(i)).
Under passive Gaussian innovations the bands are eventually left, so exact equality fails; but for a
record ofTdates, Theorem S6.2(iii) bounds the total-variation distance between the band-linear
and the linear laws byTKe −1/(2ϑ2), andϑ→0 as the capacities grow with the merged weights
fixed. A procedure with errorsα,βon the band-linear pair therefore has errors at mostα+δ,β+δ
on the linear pair, for anyδ >0, and the passive bound applies with 1−α−β−2δin place of
1−α−β.
What this changes, and what it does not.Part (ii) is the general form of Theorems S2.3
and S6.4: prices identify the aggregate flow map and nothing finer, for any population. For linear
rules the map is the kernel, which determines the merged measure; for odd saturating shapes with
independent dilations it determines the signed capacity measure; for a cubic shape it determines
only two leverage moments. Which group-level quantities prices reveal is therefore a property of the
family of rules, and it can be read off from how the family maps to flow maps. Part (iii) says that
allowing richer rules cannot make composition cheaper to learn than in the linear case, because the
hard linear pair remains in the class. What is not proved is an upper bound for general classes: for
some families composition is not identified at all (the cubic shape), and for others the attainable
rate is open.
S-24

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
Machine check.Parts (i) and (ii) are machine-checked for an arbitrary
flow map:p1 flow market invariance(Paper1SaturatingSufficiency.lean);
p1 general identification,p1 general identification iff,p1 responses of flow eqand
p1 identified functional(Paper1GeneralIdentification.lean). The band-linear embedding
in part (iii) usesp1 sat follows linear. The transfer of the lower bounds is on paper. Numeri-
cally, for a population that switches between a trend-following and a contrarian strategy according
to past profits, the steering probe of the proof drives the market to a random target path to machine
precision and recovers the flow map to 2×10−16, while a population with a different switching inten-
sity departs from the path (research_extension/theorem_checks/mc_stress_and_general.py).
What was open, and what still is.Three questions were open in earlier versions, and the
supplement answers each in part. Rates for the higher orders of Theorem S6.4: information about
the order-nterm grows like the probe size to the power 2(2n+ 1) (Companion Proposition C3.2).
Equilibrium with forward-looking dealers: the cascade threshold, its equilibria and the path of
the fall with slow-moving capital (Companion Theorem C1.1 and Companion Propositions C1.2
and C1.3). Learning agents whose rules change continuously: prices reveal their aggregate flow
kernel and nothing finer, and nonlinearity lowers the cost of learning composition only when it
separates the groups (Companion Propositions C3.6 and C3.5). Still open are dealers with richer
constraints than a constant holding cost and upper bounds for nonlinear traders who share a
leverage. Related ideas appear in the literature on funding constraints and liquidity spirals (Brun-
nermeier and Pedersen, 2009) and in evidence that crowding predicts tail risk (Barroso et al.,
2022). The identification of sums of nonlinear branches is a known problem in system identification
(Dreesen et al., 2017). What is new here is the sufficient measure inside a strategic market, the
exact absence of calm-market information about it, and the reduction of its recovery under stress
to a known uniqueness problem.
S7 Scope and limits
Linearity.Sufficiency (Theorem S2.1, Proposition S2.2) needs aggregate feedback flow to be
a linear function of past public signals, plus independent idiosyncratic shocks if there are any.
With group-specific Gaussian demand shocks, a second aggregate enters, the spectral density of
the aggregate shock process, and the equivalence class shrinks accordingly (Supplement S9). The
passive results of Section S4 are stated for Gaussian innovations, the least favourable case: for
any news law whose location divergence is quadratically bounded the passive orders are unchanged
and the constant improves by the factorσ 2I∗f≥1 (Supplement Proposition S16.2). Laws outside
that class, such as news with bounded support, are not analysed. Saturating rules are treated in
Section S6. Other nonlinear rules, and traders who change their rules as they learn, are outside
the theorems. Niroula et al. (2026a) report simulations of stylized nonlinear rules and of online
learners trained in the closed loop. In those simulations, the costs of Section S4 hold when the
competing populations’ feedback weights match. Populations whose learned weights differ are told
apart by those weights instead. Hard thresholds hide groups from small probes altogether. These
are simulations, not theorems.
Exponential memory.The identification and cost results need memory to be a finite mixture
of exponentials. For general completely monotone memory, Niroula et al. (2026a) prove that the
minimax accuracy for the memory distribution is of exact order 1/log(N/σ 2). Halving the error
then squares the data needed, instead of multiplying it by a constant.
S-25

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
The observer.The lower bounds hold for an observer who knows (a,κ) and the noise law, so
they hold a fortiori without that knowledge. The upper bound of Theorem S4.2 also holds in one
running market from an unknown state, with random-sign probes and news of any law with finite
variance (Supplement Theorem S16.1); the other upper bounds are stated for reset experiments.
Strategic responses to being observed are modelled in Proposition S5.7 and Supplement Section S15
(traders who learn from prices, and a game of surveillance and hiding). Everywhere else the groups
do not change their rules when probed.
Evidence.Niroula et al. (2026a) report pre-specified tests on two sets of trader-class reports:
the CFTC’s weekly Commitments of Traders in twenty futures markets and Binance’s class ratios
in 30 perpetual markets. Of 23 recorded outcomes, 11 pass. None is a direct test of a result in
this paper: all use class positions or account ratios rather than executed flow tagged by class. The
class reports constrain the persistence of class positions far more tightly than prices do. They
do not identify learning memory, because that persistence can also reflect turnover, reporting
aggregation or inventory retention. A pre-registered out-of-sample test of the feedback premise
on Taiwan’s tagged exchange flows (spec P1T, holdout 2019–2023; Niroula et al. (2026a) report
every diagnostic) gives the following.D= 0.0426 [0.0206,0.0643],p= 0.000, meets the registered
supportive criterion. The pre-specified reverse-causality check givesD=−0.0065 [−0.0316,0.0165],
which does not meet it. The registered verdict is ‘supportive’ . Read with its pre-specified diagnostic,
the test does not provide evidence of feedback beyond prices revealing past flow. We do not claim
that tagged flow in Taiwan responds to price deviations. The registered test is supportive, but its
pre-specified diagnostic attributes the gain to reverse causality.
A second test, of the two-layer prediction of Section S6, is registered on Binance perpetual
futures (spec P7S, amendment A1). Its primary sample is retrospective (July 2024 to October 2026),
specified in November 2026 after the sample period, and a prospective window from November 2026
serves as a replication. The primary test was run once on 11 November 2026. A pre-specified test on
Binance perpetual futures was inconclusive (calm difference−0.002, 90% interval [−0.007,0.004];
difference-in-differences 0.041,p= 0.553). It neither supports nor contradicts the prediction of
Section S6. The prospective replication covers 16 November 2026 to 14 February 2027; its symbols
and thresholds were fixed on its own development data (August to October 2026) before the window
opened, and its result is not yet available.
Open problems.Supplement Section S17 settles several earlier open questions: a long-lived
insider cannot reveal or manipulate the groups’ memory in a stable market, and a rational market
maker nets feedback out (Companion Proposition C2.1); a cascade after a breach has an exact
threshold under mechanical impact (Companion Theorem C1.1); dead-band traders are identifiable
by large probes (Companion Proposition C3.1). Supplement Section S17 also gives forward-looking
dealers (several equilibria above the threshold; Companion Proposition C1.2), saturation of every
order (Companion Proposition C3.2), a parametric rate for noisy stress identification with smooth
shapes (Companion Proposition C3.3) and the information content of learning traders (Companion
Proposition C3.6). It also covers a group that chooses its rule against an insider (Companion Propo-
sition C2.2), the path of a cascade, including slow-moving capital (Companion Proposition C1.3),
a parametric rate under hard limits (Companion Proposition C3.4), the cost of composition when
traders are nonlinear (Companion Proposition C3.5), and the largest-weight comparison for every
K(Proposition S17.2). What remains open: uniqueness of the insider game and pure equilibria
without quasi-concavity; cascades whose dealers face richer constraints than a constant holding
cost; hard-limit configurations whose kinks coincide; upper bounds for nonlinear traders who share
S-26

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
a leverage; and a supervisory loss function estimated from real interventions, which would turn the
lower bound on the value of disclosure in Supplement Corollary S16.3 into an estimate.

Proof.N p ≍σ 2(∆η)−(4K−2) by Theorems S4.1 and S4.2.Upper bounds.Average theNprice

S9 Equilibrium: further results and proofs
Corollary S9.1(Disclosure in equilibrium).Take a positive-impact equilibrium of Theorem S3.1
with positive weights andD>max iri.
(i) The groups’ flows obeyu i,t+1 =r iui,t +wixt with theirtrueweights. The price is the stable
closed loop of Theorem S3.2, driven byξ, with positive effective weights. So Proposition S5.2
applies: tagged flow identifies each group’s(r i,wi), with a limiting covariance that has no
singularity as rates collide.
(ii) On classes in which both the true and the effective weights lie in compact positive intervals,
Theorem S5.3 holds in equilibrium. Tagging cuts the experiments needed by a factor of order
(∆η)−(4K−2) for weights and(∆η) −(4K−4) for rates.
In words.What makes disclosure valuable survives the dealers’ optimization. Prices still hide
the groups at the same polynomial cost, and each group’s own flow still reveals that group’s memory
and weight directly.
Theorem S9.2(Fragility: where liquidity disappears).Let there be one feedback group with weight
w >0and memory rater∈(0,1), with¯a∈[0,1)andσ F,σξ >0. Call an equilibrium of

•A positive-impact equilibrium exists if and only ifA≤A ∗(w).

the threshold of Theorem S3.1(ii).
(iii)Bounds and rate.For everyw>0,

In words.With positive feedback, dealers can make a market only if their dislike of risk is
below a threshold, and the threshold falls as feedback grows: for strong feedback it is inversely
proportional to the feedback intensity. Past the threshold, no price impact is consistent with
dealers’ optimization. Below it, every equilibrium has stable prices. Liquidity does not erode
through growing instability; it disappears when the two equilibria merge, at a point that stable
prices give no warning of. At the calibration of Supplement S9 (¯a= 0.6,r= 0.7,σ 2F = 1,
σ2ξ = 0.25),A ∗ is 0.40 without feedback, 0.25 atG(1) = 115 and 0.034 atG(1) = 23, andwA ∗
approaches Φ∗ = 0.00688. The necessary conditions behind parts (i) and (iii) are machine-checked
(Paper1EquilibriumReducedForm.lean). With several groups, part (iv) gives a threshold that
depends on the population only through its total feedback intensityG(1). At the same calibration,
numerically, the exact thresholds for two and three groups lie between 28% and 86% of that bound,
and the equilibria on the threshold curve are stable. Every such equilibrium is stable (part (iv)),
as the scan also shows.
Theorem S9.3(A strategic insider in the inventory equilibrium).Add three things to the model
of Theorem S3.1:
•News.The fundamental moves byF t+1 =F t +θt +εt+1, withθ t∼N(0,σ 2θ)andε t i.i.d. of
varianceσ 2ε.
•An insider.At dateta short-lived insider privately observesθ t, and nothing else, submitsd t,
and values the position atF t+1.

•What groups and dealers see.The groups trade on the observed deviations t =p t−Ft. The
dealers price from total flow.
Letν t =ξ t +dt be the unexpected flow.
(i)Characterization.The strategyd t =bθ t and the pricep t =F t +λνt +xt, withx t the price
law of Theorem S3.1, form an equilibrium exactly when the induced dynamics are stable and
D= 1−κG(D), κ(D−¯a) =AVD, λ= bσ2θ
σ2ν , b= D2(κ+λ) ,
σ2ν =σ 2ξ +b 2σ2θ, V=σ 2ε + σ2θσ2ξσ2ν +(κ+λD )2σ2ν.
One unit of unexpected flow moves the price by(κ+λ)/D, and the insider’s expected profit
per date isbσ 2θ/2.
(ii)Kyle limit.Without inventory risk (A= 0),κ= 0,D= 1,λ=σ θ/(2σξ)andb=σ ξ/σθ.
These are Kyle’s one-period values at every date, and the feedback groups do not move prices.
(iii)Invariance.The coefficients depend on the groups only throughG, as Theorem S2.1 requires.
(iv)Observer.The observed deviation follows
st =a∗st−1 +κ∗∑
i
˜ui,t +c 0νt−c 1νt−1, c 0 = κ+λD , c 1 = ¯aλD,
with(a ∗,κ∗,˜u)as in Theorem S3.2. This is the closed loop of Section S1 driven through a
known moving-average filterc 0−c 1z−1, which is invertible becausec 1<c 0.
•The memory rates remain the transmission zeros of the feedback part.
•The lower-bound pair of Theorem S4.1 is realized by two such equilibria with identical
primitives.
•So the identification boundary and the(∆η) −(4K−2) rate hold with an insider present.
In words.A short-lived insider who knows the next piece of news trades against noise traders
and against the feedback traders’ predictable flow. Dealers net the predictable flow out. So the
insider’s trades move prices through two channels: an information partλand an inventory part,
both amplified by feedback throughD. Positive feedback raises the total impact. The insider then
trades less and earns less, and prices reveal less of her news. Negative feedback does the opposite.
•An example from Supplement S9.One positive-feedback group lowers the share of the insider’s
information impounded in prices from 0.372 to 0.366. One negative-feedback group raises it
to 0.415.
•What prices cannot identify.It is still worth nothing to anyone trading on public information.
•Machine check.Paper1EquilibriumInsider.leanchecks the observer’s pathwise reduced
form, the insider’s best response, the Kyle limit and the posterior variance.
•Scope.The insider’s information is short-lived, and she conditions only on her news. Two
cases are not solved: an insider who also saw the public state, who would trade against the
predictable mispricing; and a long-lived Kyle insider, who would also time her trades against
the groups’ memory.

On an unbounded horizon, equality of allk m =
∫
rmdM(r) is equality of all moments of two
finite signed measures on [0,1]. Polynomials are dense inC[0,1], so the measures coincide. In terms
ofηthis is the condition (9) of Theorem S2.3.
Part (iii).Fix a strategy profile and a realization. We argue by strong induction ont≤T. If
prices and flows agree beforet:
•the signals agree beforet, becauseS j depends on prices up toj;
•the aggregate flows attagree, by part (i) and the equal kernels, sincef t uses signals before
tonly;
•the insider’s orders attagree, by causality;
•hence the total flowsy t agree, and then the pricesp t agree, by causality of the dealer’s rule.
The insider’s profit ∑t<T (v−p t)xt and the dealer’s inventory− ∑t<Tyt are functions of these
paths.
Strategy sets are the same in the two games, because the rules are defined on the same histories.
Every payoff is an expectation of the same function of (v,z), so best responses coincide and so do
the equilibria.
Part (iv).The Gauss rule has weights 12, 12 and nodes±σwithσ 2 = 13. The Radau rule has
weights 14, 34 and nodes−1, 13. Both integrate 1,s,s 2 exactly, so on ratesc+dsthe kernels agree
form≤2. The largest weights are 12 and 34.

•both directions of part (ii) on a finite horizon:p1 eqAggFlow of kerneland
p1 kernel of eqAggFlow;

•part (iv):p1 eq group level differs.
The unbounded-horizon moment statement and the passage from pathwise equality to equal equi-
libria are on paper.
Group-specific demand shocks.Suppose groupialso trades an idiosyncratic shockϵ i,t
through its memory, so thatf t gains the term ∑iwi∑m<trmi ϵi,t−1−m. The same induction shows
that the equilibrium depends on the groups only through the kernel and the law of this aggregate
shock process. For independent Gaussian shocks with variancesσ 2i , that law is determined by the
spectral density ∑iw2iσ2i|1−r ieiω|−2. This is a second merged object, and the invariance class
shrinks accordingly. The paper’s groups have no idiosyncratic shocks.
The textbook benchmark.Take a risk-neutral, competitive dealer that knows the kernel. It
can computef t from public prices and price onlyy t−ft, so predictable feedback flow then has no
price impact, and the closed loop of Section S1 is absent in that equilibrium. The theorem holds
in that benchmark as well. Feedback reaches prices in equilibrium when the dealer bears inventory
risk, or when it does not know the kernel and must learn it. These are the cases studied in the
equilibrium extension.

largest gap 1.8×10 −4 at date five and 1.9×10 −2 by date twenty;
•the largest group’s share of feedback positions stays at medians of 0.59 and 0.79.

Proof of Theorem S9.3.Part (i).
•Dealers’ information.The groups’ flow is predictable. So the dealers’ information aboutθ t
isν t, andE[θ t|ν t] =λν t withλ= Cov(θ,ν)/Var(ν) =bσ 2θ/σ2ν.

•The price decomposes.E tFt+1 =F t +λν t andE tνt+1 = 0, soE tpt+1−p t =E txt+1−x t.
The dealers’ condition is therefore the one of Theorem S3.1. Its state is (H t,u·,t+1), and
Etui,t+2 =r iui,t+1 +wiEtxt+1. Coefficient matching gives the same equations forβ,Dand

implicit form isDx t =−κH t +∑iβiriui,t + (1−D)λν t. The response ofs t toν t is therefore
(κ+ (1−D)λ)/D+λ= (κ+λ)/D.
•The insider.F t+1−p t =θ t +ε t+1−m t−cξ t−cd t, wherec= (κ+λ)/Dandm t is the
predictable part ofs t. The insider does not observe the public state, andm t has mean zero
and is independent ofθ t. So she maximizes (θ−cd)d. Sod=θ/(2c) and the profit isθ 2/(4c)
(p1_insider_best_response). Taking expectations,bσ 2θ/2.
•The variance.The innovation ofp t+1 given dealers’ information is (θt−λνt) +εt+1 +cν t+1,
which givesV(p1_insider_posterior_variance).
•Converse.A solution of the system satisfies every condition just used.
Part (ii).WithA= 0,κ(D−¯a) = 0 forcesκ= 0, and thenD= 1. Thenb= 1/(2λ) and
λσ2ν =bσ 2θ give 4λ2σ2ξ =σ 2θ (p1_insider_kyle_limit).
Part (iii).The groups enter only throughD= 1−κG(D).
Part (iv).
•The reduced form.Combine the implicit form att, the price law att−1 andH t = ¯aHt−1−∑
iui,t−νt, as in Theorem S3.2. This givesDs t−¯ast−1 =∑iβi(D−¯a)ui,t+(κ+λ)νt−¯aλνt−1
(p1_insider_reduced_form).
•The filter is invertible.c 1 < c0 because ¯a <1,λ≥0 andκ≥0. So the observer, knowing
the primitives, inverts the filter, and is left with the closed loop of Theorem S3.2.
•Realization.Proceed as in Theorem S3.2(iv):
–setD= 1−κ ∗W/(1−a ∗), ¯a=a∗Dandκ=κ ∗D;
–solve the insider’s pair (λ,b) given (κ,D);
–setA=κ(D−¯a)/(VD).
The two economies then share every primitive.

How the solver finds equilibria.
•For one group withw >0, every equilibrium withκ >0 andD >0 hasD∈(max(¯a,r),1)
andκ= (1−D)(D−r)/w. A scan overDtherefore finds all of them.
•For several groups, the solver follows the branch ofD(κ) that is continuous withD= 1.
•Each solution is checked by simulating 2×10 5 periods of the closed loop. The check computes
the dealers’ first-order residual and the innovation variance of the price.

(ii).

A probe changes inventory as well as price. We therefore specify cash and execution timing.
Price has units of currency per asset, andκcurrency per squared quantity.
Lemma S10.2(Fair execution).For a quote schedule refreshed after the uncontrolled state update,
execution at the average price of a linear book costs, relative to the constant fundamental mark,
C(v) = 12v⊤Thv+c∥v∥ 1,(T h)ij =h|i−j|.(17)
In the positive stable economyT h is positive definite, so no nonzero round trip produces a deter-
ministic gain.
Positivity here means that the response is a positive mixture of decaying exponentials
(Lemma S8.1), whose spectral density is positive. Positive, decreasing taps alone do not suffice:
forh= (1,0.9,0.1) the 3×3 matrixT h has determinant−0.468, so some round trip earns money.
The conclusion concerns modelled execution cost only.
We verify the ledger identity (17) against an explicit cash account to 5.7×10 −13 on 1,000
random probes, and the smallest eigenvalue ofT h over 1,000 random stable positive economies is
0.141. A direct-only proxy ∑ 12κv2t omits cross-time impact: forv= (+1,−1) it gives 0.80 against
a true Toeplitz loss ofh 0−h 1 = 0.3488.

change requires a larger trade (see the disclosure contracts of Niroula et al. (2026a)).

S11 Two unknown groups: pole fitting and the global
two-point bound
Because the memory rates are zeros ofH(Theorem S2.3), extracting poles directly from the
price impulse response by matrix-pencil or Prony methods targets closed-loop eigenvalues. A
three-step inversion repairs this: (1) compute a minimal realization ofHitself from the Hankel
matrix ofh 1,h 2,...(withh 0 as the direct term) by Kung’s eigensystem realization; (2) compute its
transmission zeros, which are the memory rates and the timing zero at 0 (transforming the sequence
first, for example toht+1−aht, cancels these zeros); (3) project reconstructed flow onto the recovered
memory filters to obtain the weights (Niroula et al., 2026a, internet appendix). Without noise this
recoversK= 0,K= 1 and well-separated two-group systems exactly; with noise, per-group
weights become unreliable once rates are close. The reason is information-theoretic, as the rest of
this section shows.
S11.1 A global two-point bound for any estimator
A Cram´ er–Rao benchmark (Niroula et al., 2026a) applies only to estimators that are unbiased
near the true parameter. Could biased, ridge, shrinkage or Bayesian estimators escape the (∆η) −6
scaling? No.
Theorem S11.1(Global two-point bound and matching upper bound,K= 2).In this theorem
wk denotes groupk’s trading intensityq k, not the merged weightq kηk of Section S1, and∆ηis the
actual spacing of the two rates. Fix total intensityq= 0.20, mean rate¯η= 0.40, target separation

θ0 = (qp−,qp +,¯η−p +∆η,¯η+p−∆η), θ 1 = (qp+,qp−,¯η−p−∆η,¯η+p +∆η).(20)
(i) Label groups in increasing order of rate, so that weight vectors are compared rate by rate (as
unordered sets the two weight vectors coincide). Both economies have spacing∆η, weight
separation∥w(θ 1)−w(θ 0)∥∞ = 2τin this labelling, and equal rate momentsM m =∑kwkηmk
form∈{0,1,2}, while
M3(θ1)−M 3(θ0) = 4τp−p+(∆η)3.(21)
Hence∥h(θ 1)−h(θ 0)∥2 = Θ(τ(∆η) 3).
(ii) For any measurable estimator (including biased, ridge, James–Stein or MAP) that reports
weights in the same rate order, the supremum being over the class of two-group economies
with total weightq, mean rate¯ηand spacing∆η,

RemarkS11.2 (The hard directions carry no risk).The two economies of Theorem S11.1 have the
same total weight∑iqi =q, so their summed price response ∑tht =κ/(1−a−κ ∑iqi) is identical,
2.353 at the calibration of Table S1: the directions in which prices are least informative about the
groups are directions in which cumulative price risk does not move. The summed response is a
linear functional of the response and is estimated at the parametric rate whatever the spacing of
the memories; the companion paper turns this into a certification theorem, a minimax-optimal
pre-trade guard and a decision value of disclosure (Niroula et al., 2026b). Identification and safety
ask different questions of the same data.

General-Kscope.The same reduction gives exponent 4K−2 for a pair whose price responses

normalized weights. Lemma S12.3 and Proposition S12.4 below supply a pair with a gap for every
K. TheK= 3 rows below are numerical examples for the Gauss and Gauss–Radau pair.
Explicit robustness condition.Smooth best responses alone do not preserve separation: the
constant smooth map Φ(M) = 0 sends both post-halt budgets toa. Instead assume that, over the
stated action and holding horizon,| ~Rj−Rj|≤εforj∈{G,R}, withR R <R G. Then
RR +ε≤¯s<R G−ε=⇒ ~RR≤¯s< ~RG.(23)
Indeed, ~RR≤R R +εand ~RG≥R G−ε. A nonempty robust window exists if 2ε < R G−R R.
This is a maintained response bound, not an equilibrium result. The same allowance must cover
the alternative uniform action. At theK= 2, ∆η= 0.10 calibration,R R = 0.532,R G = 0.569909,
and ¯s= 0.55 has margin 0.018; an allowance 0.005 fits within it.
A complete conditional cost-and-error model.Letjdenote the economy,π j its declared
prior,Hthe holding horizon,bthe surplus per active class per period,C h the implementation cost
of halting andLthe penalty per period for violating the ceiling. These are assumed accounting
primitives, not estimated social welfare. An envelopeS max supports the robust uniform action
θε = min{1,max{0,(¯s−ε−a)/(S max−a)}}, L T =HbK(1−θ ε).
This action is feasible only if its worst-case budget meets the ceiling (in particulara+ε≤¯s). For

For this check alone, assume independent scalar disclosuresZ n ∼N(R j,σ 2t ) and halt when¯Z+z 1−ασt/√N+ε≤¯s. This is an illustrative measurement contract,nota claim that class
positions or tagged flow directly observe post-halt budgets. If a world is unsafe under the response

WithK= 2,N= 32,σ t = 0.02,ε= 0.005,α= 0.05,π R = 1/2,H=b= 1,C h = 0.02,L= 10,

approximately zero in G and 0.978928 in R. Buying the price record yields loss 1.515219 at this
sample size; its optimal acquisition decision is to decline and retain the no-data action. An informed
oracle before information fees has loss 1.234609.
Across 2,304 deterministic configurations varying fees, noise, horizon, prior, sample size and
allowance, paid disclosure improves on the no-data benchmark in 1,207, worsens it in 1,079, and
ties in 18. These counts have no population or statistical-significance interpretation. Negative
cases remain in the output. A downward measurement bias of 0.03 produces false-halt probability
0.418776; a future response of 0.025 despite an allowance of 0.005 makes halting unsafe in R with
probability 0.978928. The latter also invalidates the uniform-action guarantee, so payoff compar-
isons under that misspecification are not presented as a fair safety comparison. The experiment
establishes conditional accounting and failure modes, not real-market gains, a validated equilibrium
response, or the general-Ktheorem.

Operational bridge from tagged flows to a halt decision.The scalar-reading contract
above can be replaced, within the reset model, by five observed channels: two first class-flow
responses, two second responses, and the first price response. For a unit pulse writef i,0 =κw i,
fi,1 =w i(κri +h 1), andh 1 =κa+κ 2∑iwi. Thus
wi =f i,0/κ, r i =f i,1/fi,0−h 1/κ, q i =w i/(1−r i).
Assume known impact, verified stable class membership, 0≤r i ≤0.95, independent unbiased
Gaussian reset readings with per-channel varianceσ 2f, andNindependent records. A simultaneous
radiuse=σ f
√
5/(Nα) covers the five sample means with probability at least 1−αby Chebyshev
and a union bound. Interval division, only when the first-flow lower endpoint is positive, gives
bounds onw i,ri,qi; intersect rate bounds with the declared class. Halt only if the remaining
upper budget plus the response allowance meets the ceiling. Otherwise retain the same robust
uniform fallback. On simultaneous coverage, every selected halt is safe. This supplies a conditional
observation-to-action argument without feeding the unknownR,worrto the rule.
The separate Lean modulePaper1FlowDecision.leancovers exact flow inversion, ratio and
clipped intervals, the intensity interval, selected-halt inequality, actual product-Gaussian joint mean
bound and a probability-composition theorem with an explicit rule-soundness premise. Their de-
terministic composition, clipping implementation and executable simulation still require separate
correspondence checks; no claim of a fully formalized executable decision algorithm is made. The
pinned build receipt records the checked declarations.
The exploratory scriptstudies/paper1_uplift_20261105/flow_decision.pyruns 20,000 in-
dependent replications per world in each of 60 configurations:N∈ {32,128,512,2048,8192},
σf ∈ {0.0005,0.002,0.005},H∈ {1,10}andF∈ {0.05,0.25}. Other quantities use the two-
group calibration above, a per-channel-vector record fee 0.0005, and the same class-halt or throttle
action menu. Fourteen configurations have positive estimated net information value; the others are
retained. The largest observed gain is exploratory, selected from the grid: atN= 128,σ f = 0.0005,
H= 10 andF= 0.05, no-data loss is 14.492188, information-inclusive loss 12.370094 and net gain
2.122094 surplus units. A simultaneous grid-adjusted Hoeffding interval is reported in the result
file; zero observed unsafe halts is not a proof of zero probability. Costs, horizons and response
bounds are synthetic, and no real-market gain or calibrated welfare conclusion follows. Six ad-
ditional fixed stress cases retain both smaller biases with no observed unsafe halt and failures: a
downward second-flow bias of 0.010 produces unsafe halts in G in all 20,000 replications, while a re-
sponse 0.030 exceeding the allowance 0.005 produces unsafe halts in R in all replications. Swapped
second-flow labels lead to abstention in both worlds. These are synthetic contract falsifications,
not guarantees under misspecification.

S12.1 The targeted halt for everyK
Proposition S5.1(ii) is stated for two groups. Its argument needs two populations whose kernels
agree to order (∆η) 2K−2 and whose largest groups differ at order one. This subsection supplies
such a pair for everyK.
Letµbe the uniform probability measure on [−1,1], letp 0,p 1,...be its orthonormal polyno-
mials, and putλ K(s) = 1/∑j<Kpj(s)2. Call a ruleadmissibleif it hasKdistinct nodes in [−1,1]
and integrates every polynomial of degree at most 2K−2 exactly againstµ. The Gauss rule and
the Gauss–Radau rule of Theorem S4.1 are admissible.
Lemma S12.3(Largest weights in the family).FixK≥2.
(i) An admissible rule with nodest 1,...,t K has weightsω i =λ K(ti). In particular its weights
are positive.
(ii) There isc 0 >0such that, for every|c|≤c 0, the zeros ofp K−cp K−1 are the nodes of an
admissible ruleR c. A point is a node ofR c for at most onec.
(iii) There arec 1̸=c 2 in[−c 0,c 0]for whichR c1 andR c2 have different largest weights. Their
moments of degree2K−1differ.
Proof.(i) Letℓ i be the Lagrange polynomials of the nodes. Each productℓ iℓj has degree 2K−2,
so the rule integrates it exactly:
∫
ℓiℓjdµ= ∑mωmℓi(tm)ℓj(tm) =ω i1{i=j}. Takingi=jgives
ωi =
∫
ℓ2idµ>0. Soℓ i/√ωi,i= 1,...,K, is an orthonormal basis of the polynomials of degree at
mostK−1, as isp 0,...,p K−1. The sum of squares of an orthonormal basis at a point does not
depend on the basis, so ∑j<Kpj(s)2 =∑iℓi(s)2/ωi. Ats=t i the right side is 1/ωi.
(ii) The zeros ofp K are real, simple and inside (−1,1). Simple real zeros move continuously
with the coefficients, so for|c|≤c 0, withc 0 small,q c =p K−cp K−1 hasKsimple zeros in (−1,1).
Give these nodes the interpolatory weights. Letfhave degree at most 2K−2 and writef=q cg+ρ
with degg≤K−2 and degρ≤K−1. Bothp K andp K−1 are orthogonal to polynomials of degree
at mostK−2, so
∫
qcgdµ= 0, and the rule integratesρexactly and givesq cgthe value zero.
Hence the rule is exact forf. Ifsis a zero ofq c and ofq c′ withc̸=c ′, thenp K−1(s) = 0 and so
pK(s) = 0. By the three-term recurrence a common zero ofp K andp K−1 is a zero ofp K−2, and so
on down top 0 = 1, which has none.
(iii) The reciprocal 1/λK is a polynomial of degree 2K−2≥2, so every level set ofλ K is finite.
Suppose all the rulesR c,|c|≤c 0, had the same largest weightv. By (i) eachR c would have a
node in the finite set{λ K =v}, and by (ii) different values ofcwould give different nodes. There
are infinitely manyc, so this is impossible. For the last statement, letνbe the difference of the
two rules. It is supported on at most 2Kpoints. If the two rules also agreed at degree 2K−1,ν
would vanish on every polynomial of degree at most 2K−1, including the one that equals 1 at one
support point and 0 at the others. Soνwould be zero, which contradicts (ii) and the positivity of
the weights.

hypotheses of Theorem S4.1, let economyj∈{1,2}haveKgroups with ratesr c +∆ηs ji and merged
weightsWω ji , and writeq ji =Wω ji/(1−r c−∆ηs ji).

Proof.(i) The proof of Theorem S4.1 uses four properties of its pair: nodes in [−1,1], positive

S12.3 Stress identification with noisy observations
Theorem S6.4 assumes exactly observed responses. The next result shows that the identification
survives observation noise: a minimum-distance estimator recovers the signed capacity measure
consistently. Companion Proposition C3.3 adds the rate for smooth shapes.
Proposition S12.6(Noisy stress identification).Letψbe continuous, odd and with independent
dilations. Fixn≥1,0< ℓ min≤ℓ max,0≤¯r <1and¯c >0, and letΘbe the set of populations
of at mostntraders, writtenθ= (c i,ri,ℓi)i≤n withc i∈[0,¯c],r i∈[0,¯r]andℓ min≤|ℓ i|≤ℓ max

Proof. Continuity.For a fixed probe, the response at each date is a finite composition of the
market recursion, sums, products andψ, soh j is continuous on Θ, which is compact. The map
θ↦→˜µ(θ) = ∑ici sgn(ℓi)δ (ri,|ℓi|) is continuous into the weak topology, because sgn(ℓ i) is constant
on each of the two componentsℓ i≥ℓ min andℓ i≤−ℓ min; the weak topology on this bounded set of
measures is metrized by the bounded-Lipschitz distanceρ.
Identification.LetQ(θ) = ∑j 2−j min(1,∥hj(θ0)−h j(θ)∥). IfQ(θ) = 0, the two populations
give identical responses to every rational probe sequence. Responses are continuous in the probe, by
the same composition argument, and rational sequences of each length are dense, so the responses
agree for every probe sequence. Drop the traders withc i = 0 from both populations; the remaining
traders satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem S6.4, so ˜µ(θ) = ˜µ(θ 0). Conversely ˜µ(θ) = ˜µ(θ0) gives
Q(θ) = 0 by the same theorem.

The argument is the standard one for extremum estimators: identification, compactness,
continuity and uniform convergence. Its abstract form is machine-checked on any com-
pact parameter set, in probability, together with the uniform bound for the truncated crite-
ria (p1 extremum consistency prob,p1 criterion uniform close,Paper1NoisyStress.lean);
continuity of the responses and of ˜µand the expectation bound are on paper. It inherits the hard
S-56

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
limit’s requirement of probes of all sizes; for smooth shapes, Companion Proposition C3.3 gives
the parametric rate. A Monte Carlo run with a trend follower and a contrarian of the tanh shape,
seven probes and observation noise, shows the consistency: the median parameter error falls from
0.24 with one run per probe to 0.050, 0.024, 0.0052 and 0.0009 with 16, 256, 4,096 and 65,536 runs,
close to theN −1/2 rate (research_extension/theorem_checks/mc_stress_and_general.py).
For tanh, Proposition S6.3 shows the information per probe grows like the sixth power of its
size for small probes, so rates for the full measure will depend on how far the design pushes the
market.

S14 Which questions prices answer cheaply
The cost results of Section S4 concern composition. This section asks the same question of every
linear question about the population, and finds a dichotomy: some questions are answered at the
usual statistical rate whatever the number of groups and the spacing of their memories, some cannot
be answered at all without a bound on memory, and the cost of each is the value of a problem of
polynomial approximation. The results and proofs are adapted from the first author’s thesis (in
preparation).
Setting.WriteM= ∑iwiδri for the merged measure, so thatk m =∫ rmdM, andM(¯r, ¯W) for
the positive measures on [0,¯r] of total mass at most ¯W, with ¯r <1 a memory bound. Alinear
questionisF g(M) =

mkm, which withaandκfixes the feedback budgetS=a+κΦ. The observer runsNindependent
experiments, each returning the firstLkernel taps with independentN(0,σ 2) noise. One way
to obtain them is a steering probe that holds the price at a unit impulse (as in the proof of
Theorem S6.7), with aggregate feedback flow observed: the flow at datet+ 1 is thenk t. Price
records are discussed at the end of the section. For accuracyϵ >0 and level 1−α,N cert(ϵ;Fg) is

Part (iv) has the structure of the classical theory of estimating a linear functional over a convex
class (Donoho, 1994): one modulus governs both the lower bound and an affine interval. What
is specific to this market is the dual form. The cost of a question about the traders is the value
of a problem of polynomial approximation ofgon the memory range, in which the coefficients
are penalized because each multiplies a noisy tap. The number of groups and the spacing of their
memories do not enter.

square-summable coefficients and are parametric: they cost of orderσ 2ϵ−2 experiments however

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
many groups there are and however close their memories. Square-summable power series are the
restrictions of the Hardy spaceH 2 of the unit disc, whose functions grow at most like (1−r)−1/2 as
r↑1; that is exactly the growth beyond which part (iii) rules out certification without a memory
bound. The total feedback weight Φ, which fixes the feedback budget and with it stability and
worst-case transient loss, hasa t≡1 and sits on the boundary: Niroula et al. (2026b) show that
it costsσ 2ϵ−2 log(1/ϵ) experiments and that the logarithm cannot be removed. Composition sits
at the far end. The weight of a group is not a linear functional ofMat all, and by Theorem S4.1
its cost grows like (∆η)−(4K−2). Market-wide questions are cheap; questions about who trades are
not.
Price records.The theorem is stated for observed kernel taps. With price records only, the first
T+ 1 price taps are a function of the firstTkernel taps (h 0 =κandh t+1 =ah t +κ ∑j≤tkjht−j),
so part (ii) holds withT+ 1 price taps in place ofTkernel taps, and every lower bound for kernel
taps that rests on equal taps holds for price taps. In the other direction, the map from the first
L+ 1 price taps to the firstLkernel taps is triangular, and its linearization at a stable economy is
a lower-triangular Toeplitz operator whose symbol, the reciprocal of the square of the closed-loop
transfer function up to a factor, is bounded on the unit circle uniformly inL. Estimated kernel taps
therefore haveℓ 2 errors of orderσN −1/2 to first order, and the parametric case of Proposition S14.2
carries over to price records to first order, on classes with a uniform stability margin. We do not
state the higher-order terms.
Machine check.For finite populations, the weak-duality half of (iv), the affine esti-
mate of (i), part (iii) and the parametric direction of Proposition S14.2 are machine-checked
(p1 question weak duality,p1 question truncation error,p1 long memory impossible,
p1 parametric modulus,Paper1QuestionsCost.lean). The lower half of (iv), part (ii) and the
converse of the proposition rest on Sion’s minimax theorem, duality for best uniform approximation
and weak compactness inℓ 2, and are proved on paper.
Numerical check.With two groups at memories 0.5±∆η, the error of the truncated es-
timator of the discounted feedback (β= 0.9) from noisy taps does not depend on ∆η, while
the error of the weights by Prony’s method grows like (∆η) −3: fitted slopes−0.02 and−3.02
over ∆η∈ {0.2,0.1,0.05}, 2,000 replications each (research_extension/theorem_checks/mc_
questions.py).
S15 Surveillance over time and against strategic
traders
The cost results treat the population as fixed. Real populations drift: algorithms enter, exit and are
retuned, some of them in response to the supervision itself, and the groups being watched choose
how to trade. This section gives three results: a speed limit on tracking a moving population, a
floor on what probing can learn about traders who learn from prices, and an equilibrium in which
surveillance without tags pushes algorithms together and never detects them.
S-62

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms

What this means.Theorem S4.1 says composition is expensive to learn; Theorem S15.1 says
that when composition moves, the expense becomes a speed limit. The supervisor’s error falls only
like the cube root of the data rate, and populations that change faster thanv ∗∝n(∆η) 4K−2/σ2
cannot be tracked at all. In the proof, a population that drifts across the distance between two
members of the hard family in a few identification times cannot be told from one that stays still.
At illustrative parameters (memories with half-lives of 5 to 9 days), where telling such a pair apart
takes thousands of years of daily prices, composition is untrackable unless it is stable over millennia.
Market-wide questions are tracked at the cube-root rate whatever the number of groups; and along
the hard directions their answers hardly move, because those directions preserve the low moments
that market-wide answers mostly depend on.
Numerical check.With two groups moving along the family and the window chosen to mini-
mize the median error, the weight error grows with the speed at fitted slope 0.33 (against 1/3)
and with the spacing at slope−2.01 (against−2, the cube root of (∆η) −6). The error for
the discounted feedback grows with speed at slope 0.34; its dependence on the spacing, slope
0.98, comes only through the speed of its answer, which along the family is of order (∆η) 3
(research_extension/tracking/tracking_check.py).
S15.2 Traders who learn from prices: an observer effect
Suppose the traders adjust their rules in response to what they earn, and so in response to the
supervisor’s probes. A probe of sizeAmoves a group’s position by an amount proportional to
Aand the price by an amount proportional toA, so its profit and loss is proportional toA 2:
with positionw imt and price changes ∆xt+1, the profit over a probe’s response isw iA2Πi, where
Πi = ∑tm(1)t ∆x(1)t+1 is computed on the unit response. A rule that moves its weight byηtimes
realized profit therefore moves by at mostηA 2 maxi|wiΠi|per probe. Call a class of learning rules
of sensitivityγif, withnprobes of sizeAper period, the population can move at speed up to
v0 +γnA 2, and the supervisor knows only this bound.

per period and learning rules of sensitivityγ, every probing policy leaves a minimax tracking error

leastσ 2γ(∆η)−(4K−2). The lower bound of Theorem S15.1(i) needs only that some admissible path

sensitivity allows. The market-wide case is the same computation with Theorem S15.1(iv).
Probing harder buys information at rateA 2 and moves the target at rateA 2, so the two cancel:
surveillance of traders who learn from prices has a floor that no probing design removes. Two
caveats bound the result. It is a minimax statement over learning rules of a given sensitivity;
a supervisor who knows the learning rule can anticipate the drift it causes. And the floor for
composition carries the factor (∆η)−(4K−2), while the floor for market-wide questions does not.
S15.3 A surveillance and hiding game
A group that expects to be targeted once identified may choose to trade like its neighbours. Consider
two untagged groups and let one of them choose the spacingD∈(0,D 0] between its memory and
its neighbour’s, whereD 0 is the spacing of its preferred strategy and moving away from it costs
k(D0−D) 2. A supervisor withNobservations runs a test with false-alarm probabilityα; by
Theorems S4.1 and S4.4, its detection probability depends on the data and the spacing through
ND 6 at leading order,π(ND 6) withπcontinuous and nondecreasing andπ(0) =α. Detection
costs the groupL>0. Tags, if the supervisor imposes them, make the group’s weight identifiable
at a cost that does not depend onD(Theorem S5.4).
Proposition S15.3(Surveillance and hiding).LetD ∗(N)minimizek(D 0−D) 2 +Lπ(ND 6)over
(0,D 0].
(i)Crowding.Ifπis differentiable atND 60 with positive derivative, thenD ∗(N)<D 0.
(ii)Surveillance without tags is self-limiting. lim sup N→∞π(ND∗(N) 6)≤α+kD 20/L. When
the stakes are high,L≫kD 20, the group is detected about as often as a false alarm, however
much data the supervisor has.
(iii)Tags remove crowding.If the detection probability does not depend onD, thenD ∗ =D 0.
(iv)Many groups.LetM≥2untagged groups choose memory positionsp g, groupgpaying
kg(pg−p◦g)2 +L gπ(ND 6g), wherep ◦g is its preferred position andD g its distance to the
nearest other group. In every Nash equilibrium (existence is not established here) in which
the distance∆ g fromp ◦g to the nearest other group’s position stays bounded asNgrows,
lim supN→∞π(ND 6g)≤α+k g lim supN ∆2g/Lg for every group.
Proof.(i) The derivative of the objective atD 0 is 6LND 50π′(ND 60)>0, so a small move below
D0 lowers it. (ii) For anyc >0 andNlarge enough thatcN −1/6 ≤D 0, optimality against
D=cN −1/6 givesLπ(ND ∗6)≤k(D 0−cN−1/6)2 +Lπ(c 6)≤kD 20 +Lπ(c 6). Lettingc→0 and
using continuity ofπat zero gives the bound. (iii) The costk(D 0−D) 2 is minimized atD 0. (iv)
Holding the others’ positions fixed, groupgcan move to withincN −1/6 of its nearest neighbour, at
cost at mostk g(∆g +cN−1/6)2 and detection probability at mostπ(c 6), becauseπis nondecreasing.
Equilibrium givesL gπ(ND 6g)≤k g(∆g +cN−1/6)2 +Lgπ(c6); lettingN→∞and thenc→0 gives
the bound.
The proposition gives the paper’s results on cost an equilibrium reading. More data without
tags make high-stakes algorithms trade alike, and so make composition harder to learn, while the
detection probability stays near the false-alarm level. Tags undo the crowding. Two predictions
follow that algorithm-level data could test: where algorithm identification is introduced, the disper-
sion of memory rates across algorithm classes should rise; and in untagged markets, the memories
S-65

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
of strategies with the most to lose from targeting should converge as surveillance improves. With
the Gaussian test, the optimal spacing in the high-stakes case falls likeN−1/4 and detection stays at
the 5% false-alarm level up toN= 10 16; in the low-stakes case the group hides for moderateNand
stops hiding once data are plentiful (research_extension/tracking/hiding_game_check.py).
With several groups, part (iv) bounds detection group by group in every equilibrium; whether an
equilibrium exists, and what it looks like, is not studied here.
Machine check.The divergence of a moving window, Le Cam’s two-point step, the bias–noise
trade-off, the observer-effect inequality and parts (ii)–(iv) of Proposition S15.3 are machine-
checked (p1 track kl window,p1 track two point,p1 track tradeoff,p1 observer floor,
p1 hiding detection bound,p1 hiding tag no crowding,Paper1TrackingGame.lean;
p1 hiding nash bound,Paper1NewsLawHiding.lean). The analytic properties of the family
Rc, the moment Lipschitz bounds and the closed-loop step are on paper.
S16 One running market, news of any law, and the
value of tags in money
The upper bounds above are stated for reset experiments, and the passive bounds for Gaussian
news. This section removes both restrictions for the composition cost and turns the cost into money.
Probing a market that has been running from an unknown state needs the same order of data as
reset experiments; Gaussian news is the least favourable news law, and any other law in a broad
class changes only the constant; and the cost of learning composition from prices is a lower bound
on the money value of class tags.

S16.2 News of any law
Let the news be independent with a densityfof mean zero and varianceσ 2, and writeD f(∆) =∫
f(x) log f(x)f(x−∆) dxfor its location divergence. Say thatfhasquadratic location divergencewith
constantI ∗f ifD f(∆)≤ 12I∗f ∆2 for all ∆. Gaussian news hasI ∗f = 1/σ 2, with equality. Laplace
news with scalebhasD f(∆) =|∆|/b−1 +e −|∆|/b≤∆ 2/(2b2), soσ 2I∗f = 2; Student-tnews with
five degrees of freedom hasσ 2I∗f = 1.25, computed numerically.
Proposition S16.2(News of any law).(i) Populations with the same kernel produce the same
law of prices for every news law; populations with different kernels produce different laws for
every news law with finite variance.
(ii) Iffhas quadratic location divergence, then for any two economies and any record lengthT,
the divergence between their price records from rest satisfiesKL f ≤σ 2I∗f·KL Gauss, where
KLGauss is the divergence under Gaussian news of the same variance. The passive lower
bounds of Theorem S4.4 therefore hold with the number of observations divided byσ 2I∗f : the
order(∆η)−(4K−2) is the same.
(iii)σ 2I∗f≥1, with equality for Gaussian news. Gaussian news is the least favourable law in this
class.
Proof.(i) Prices are the closed-loop filter of the news, and the filter is determined by the kernel
(Theorem S2.1); different kernels give different autocovariances whenever the news has finite vari-
ance. (ii) The filter from news to prices has leading coefficient one and a causal, stable inverse,
εt =x t−axt−1−κ∑mkmxt−1−m, so from rest the record determines the news exactly, and under
economyθthe density of the record is ∏tf(xt−mθt ) withm θt a linear function of past prices. The
chain rule gives KLf =∑t EADf(∆t) with ∆t =m Bt −mAt . Under economyA, ∆ t is a linear func-
tion of past news, soE A∆2t depends on the news law only throughσ 2, and the same identity under
Gaussian news reads KL Gauss = ∑t EA∆2t/(2σ2). Hence KL f ≤ 12I∗f∑t EA∆2t =σ 2I∗f KLGauss.
(iii) Near zero,D f(∆) = 12I(f)∆ 2 +o(∆ 2), withI(f) the Fisher information for location, so
I∗f ≥I(f)≥1/σ 2 by the Cram´ er–Rao inequality for location, with equality only for the normal
law.
S-68

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
Laws outside the class, for example news with bounded support, whose shifted densities are
mutually singular, can make prices far more informative; we do not analyse them. For the two-
group pair of Theorem S4.1 and records of 200 periods from rest, the ratio KL f/KLGauss is 1.999
under Laplace news (σ2I∗f = 2) and 1.250 under Student-tnews with five degrees of freedom (1.25):
the pair is close, so the bound is attained to three digits (research_extension/running/running_
check.py).

S16.3 The value of tags in money
Corollary S16.3(A lower bound on the value of tags).In the setting of Theorem S4.1, let each
experiment costc e. Any procedure that tells the Gauss and Gauss–Radau pair apart from prices
with error probabilitiesα 0 +β 0<1costs at leastcc eσ2(1−α 0−β 0)2(∆η)−(4K−2). With class tags,
by Theorem S5.3(i), recovering the weights costs at mostCc e, independently of∆η. The money
value of tags to a supervisor who needs composition, measured as the cost they save, is therefore at
leastc e
(
cσ 2(1−α 0−β 0)2(∆η)−(4K−2)−C).
The cost of an experiment is the execution cost of its probe, priced in Section S10. The corollary
gives a floor, not an estimate: the value of tags to a supervisor who never needs composition is
zero, and the corollary says nothing about the supervisor’s loss from acting without it.
Machine check.The first statement is machine-checked for the moment-matched pair that Lean
uses for Theorem S4.1 (binomial weights on equally spaced nodes, the same order), by composing
the cost with the uniform lower bound (p1 tags value money,Paper1NewsLawHiding.lean); the
tagged upper bound is that of Theorem S5.3.

Radau weights are πK√1−x 2(1+O(K−2)), and the Gauss weights are πK+1/2√1−x 2(1+O(K−2)).

S18 General memory: the minimax rate is logarithmic
Exponential memory is a modelling choice. Suppose instead that the feedback kernel is completely
monotone,k m =
∫
[0,ρ]rmdµ(r) withρ<1, whereµis any nonnegative measure of total massW: a
finite or infinite mixture of exponential memories with merged weightW. WriteM(ρ,W) for this
class, assume the closed loop is uniformly stable,s≡a+κW/(1−ρ)<1, and measure accuracy
by
d(µ,µ′) = sup{∫ fd(µ−µ ′) :f1-Lipschitz on [0,ρ], f(0) = 0 },
which equals the Wasserstein-1 distance between measures of equal mass and also compares esti-
mates whose mass is not exactlyW. LetE L(|x|) denote the error of the best uniform approximation
of|x|on [−1,1] by polynomials of degreeL, and letc ∗ = infL≥1LEL(|x|). Bernstein (1914) showed
thatLE L(|x|) converges to a positive constant (0.2802; Varga and Carpenter, 1985), soc ∗ >0;
numericallyc∗ = 1/4, attained atL= 2 (Table S2).
Lemma S18.1(Moment-matched group pairs).For everyn≥2there are two probability measures
on[0,ρ], each with exactlynatoms, whose moments of orders0,...,2n−2coincide and whose
Wasserstein-1 distance is at leastρE 2n−2(|x|)≥c ∗ρ/(2n−2).
The atoms are the points where the best approximation of|r−ρ/2|by polynomials of degree
2n−2 attains its maximal error with alternating signs, and the weights are those of the divided
difference on these points, which annihilates every polynomial of degree 2n−2 (Supplement S8).
Each measure is ann-atom merged measure in the class; one atom may sit at rate 0 (a group with
η= 1), which the class allows.

N/σ2 beyond a threshold depending only on (a,κ,ρ,s,W,β). The formal constants are larger than
the theorem’s 3πandB(ρ), because the formal deconvolution bound grows geometrically inL; the
order is the same.
Theorem S18.2 replaces a polynomial barrier with a logarithmic one. With exponential mem-
ory andKknown groups, halving the error multiplies the required number of experiments by a
constant; with general memory, halving the error squares it. The lower bound holds already for
finite mixtures, so knowing that the memory is a finite mixture of exponentials does not help unless
the number of groups is also known (Theorem S19.1). The constants of the two bounds differ,
by a factor that grows asρ→1, so the dependence on how long memory lasts is not pinned
down. The source of the gap is visible in the proof: asρ→1 the moment-matched pairs separate
their kernels more slowly (ρ 2n−1 decays slowly), which weakens the lower bound through log(1/ρ),
while the upper bound pays logB(ρ), which stays bounded away from zero, for the conditioning of
polynomial approximation on [0,ρ]. Logarithmic rates of this kind are familiar from deconvolution
with supersmooth errors (Fan, 1991) and from moment methods for mixing distributions (Kong
and Valiant, 2017; Wu and Yang, 2020); here they arise from the closed loop of a market.
Table S2 checks the construction and the estimator. The pairs of Lemma S18.1 have exactly
natoms, agree in all moments below order 2n−1 to 10 −61, and remain at Wasserstein distance
about 0.4/n, while the distance between their kernels shrinks by a factor of about 0.2 per atom at
ρ= 0.9. The linear-programming estimator applied to a continuous memory distribution has error
times log(N/σ 2) between 0.18 and 0.28 forNfrom 10 4 to 1024: a 10 20-fold increase in data halves
the error. The exponential-memory model of the previous subsections, where the cost is polynomial
in 1/∆η, is therefore the optimistic case: relaxing the parametric form makes identification from
prices much harder, not easier.
Full proofs.
