S19 Near-colliding rates: a uniform rate for the
merged measure
Theorem S4.2 needs the rates to stayγ-separated and every weight to stay away from zero. Neither
restriction can be dropped for weight recovery: two atoms of weightswandW−wat ratesrand
r+ ∆ and two of weightsW−wandwat the same rates have merged measures that converge
to the same measure as ∆→0, so no estimator recovers the individual weights uniformly over
near-colliding economies. What can be recovered uniformly is the merged measure itself, in the
permutation-invariant Wasserstein distance.
Theorem S19.1(Uniform rate without separation).FixK≥1, a memory bound¯r <1and
0< mmin≤B. LetM K be the merged measures with at mostKatoms in[0,¯r]and total mass
k0∈[m min,B], with no separation condition and no lower bound on individual weights, and write
¯M=M/k 0 for the normalized memory distribution. FromNreset experiments as in Theorem S4.2,
let ˆMbe any element ofM K whose first2Kkernel values are closest in the sup norm to the
estimated ones. There isCdepending only on(K,¯r,a,κ,B)such that, with probability at least
1−β,
W1( ˆ¯M, ¯M)≤C (σ√log(4K/β)mmin√N )1/(2K−1) uniformly overM K.
HenceN=O(σ 2m−2minϵ−(4K−2))experiments give accuracyϵ, and with the Wasserstein statement
of Theorem S4.1 the minimax rate for the merged memory distribution isN −1/(4K−2), uniformly
over near-colliding economies.
Proof.As in the proof of Theorem S4.2, the kernel valuesk 0,...,k 2K−1 are a Lipschitz function of
the first 2K+1 responses on the bounded set of responses the class produces, so a union bound gives
maxt<2K|¯kt−kt|≤e=C 1σ√log(4K/β)/Nwith probability at least 1−β(the Lipschitz constant is
for a bounded neighbourhood of the class; when the noise is too large for that, the right-hand side of
the theorem exceeds ¯r≥W1 onceCis enlarged, so the bound holds trivially). The class is compact
and the moment map continuous, so a closest element ˆMexists, and max t|kt( ˆM)−k t(M)|≤2e
by the triangle inequality. Since all atoms lie in [0,1],|k t|≤k 0, and the normalized moments
mt =k t/k0 satisfy|m t( ˆM)−m t(M)| ≤2e/k0 + (|ˆkt|/ˆk0)(2e/k0)≤4e/m min. The affine map
r↦→2r/¯r−1 carries both normalized measures to distributions on [−1,1] with at mostKatoms,
multipliesW 1 by 2/¯rand the differences of moments of order below 2Kby at most (1 + 2/¯r)2K−1.
The moment comparison inequality forK-atomic distributions on [−1,1] (Wu and Yang, 2020),
W1≤c Kδ1/(2K−1) when the first 2K−1 moments differ by at mostδ, then gives the bound with
C=c K(¯r/2)(1 + 2/¯r)(4C1)1/(2K−1).
S-75

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
The theorem separates two questions that Theorem S4.2 answers together. Which memory
distribution do the participants have? That has the same polynomial cost whether or not their rates
collide. Which participant has which weight? That becomes arbitrarily expensive as rates approach
each other (Theorem S4.1) and impossible when they coincide (Theorem S2.3). A supervisor who
needs the first can have it at the rate of Theorem S4.1; one who needs the second has to be told
the number of groups and a separation, or has to obtain tagged data (Section S5).

S22 Do the rates survive real market noise?
Theorems S4.1 and S4.2 are proved for observation errors that are independent and Gaussian, and
Niroula et al. (2026a) checks them with Gaussian noise. Real price changes are different: one-minute
returns have heavy tails, although they are almost uncorrelated from one minute to the next. To
see whether the rates depend on the Gaussian assumption, we ran a pre-specified test (opened once)
in which the trader groups are synthetic, with known memory spacing, and only the noise is real.
Each reset experiment returns the price response to a unit impulse plus a window of consecutive
one-minute log-price changes of Binance BTCUSDT, demeaned and scaled to unit variance. The
changes come from 21 days, the 6th of each month from January 2025 to September 2026, that are
used nowhere else in this project; their kurtosis is 19.4, against 3 for Gaussian noise. With class
tagging, the experiment also returns each group’s flow response, observed through independent
windows of the same noise. A control arm uses i.i.d. Gaussian noise of the same variance. For each
spacing,N ∗ is the smallest number of experiments at which every recovered weight lies within 25%
of the smallest true weight in at least 75% of 200 replications, using Prony’s method on prices alone
or the per-group regression of Proposition S5.2 with tagging. The noise level and the spacing grids
were fixed on Gaussian noise alone, before any real noise was downloaded.
Both pre-specified hypotheses hold, so the test passes (Table S4). With real noise, the price-
only requirement for two groups rises from 64 experiments at ∆η= 0.5 to 370,728 at ∆η= 0.125,
a factor of about 5,800, and the fitted slope is−6.01; for three groups it is−9.81. Over the same
grid the tagged requirement stays between 256 and 724 experiments, and it falls slightly rather
than rises as the spacing shrinks. At every spacing the real-noise requirement is within one step
of the grid (a factor
√
2) of the Gaussian control. The slopes are least-squares fits of logN ∗ on

K= 2,r c = 0.5 and ∆ηfrom 0.6 to 0.25 forK= 3),a= 0.5,κ= 0.8, total weight 0.08,σ= 10 −3,
σf = 0.02. The test passes if H1 and H2 hold; H3 is reported either way. The tagged row gives the

the text. Requirements at each spacing are in Niroula et al. (2026a). Test opened once.
log ∆ηover the stated grids (seven spacings from 0.5 to 0.125 for two groups, five from 0.6 to 0.25
for three). No uncertainty interval was pre-specified; as a post hoc description, the ordinary least-
squares standard errors are 0.17 and 0.47 with real noise (0.12 and 0.31 for the Gaussian control,
slopes−6.22 and−9.96), and eachN ∗ is itself read off a grid with steps of √2. For two groups,
the last four price-only points lie above 4,096 and use the normal draw, so the heavy tails enter the
fit only through the first three.
The test has a limit, stated in its specification. Averages ofNreal windows are resampled
exactly up toN= 4,096; beyond that they are drawn as Gaussian vectors with the real windows’
covariance, as the central limit theorem suggests. Real one-minute changes are almost uncorrelated
(lag-one autocorrelation between−0.007 and 0.009 within the windows), so beyond 4,096 the real
and Gaussian arms differ little by construction. The direct evidence about heavy tails therefore
comes from the points in the exact range: every tagged requirement, and the price-only requirements
for two groups at the three widest spacings (64, 362 and 1,448 experiments, against 64, 362 and
1,024 with Gaussian noise). The other four price-only points for two groups, and all points for three
groups, check the rate with the real noise’s variance and covariance but not its tails. Averaging
should make heavy tails matter less asNgrows, but the test does not show this.
S23 What prices hide, in economic terms
The worked example states the barrier in signal-to-noise ratios. That is the general picture. Write
s=σ 2ζ/σ2ν for a desk’s signal-to-noise ratio, so that by (8) its memory rate is 1−η(s) with
η(s) = 12(√s2 + 4s−s ), η ′(s) = 12( s+ 2√s2 + 4s−1)>0,
andηis increasing and concave. With no inventory penalty, the first-order condition of the Remark
on exponential memory gives the trading intensityq i =β i/λtc,i: what the desk expects to earn per
unit of belief, over what it pays to trade.
Proposition S23.1(Identification in economic primitives).ConsiderKKalman desks
(Lemma S1.1(i)) with signal-to-noise ratioss i∈[s ,¯s]⊂(0,∞)and intensitiesq i =β i/λtc,i, and
writec− =η′(¯s)andc + =η′(s).
(i) Prices and aggregate order flow depend on the desks only through the measure
∑

qiη(si)δ 1−η(si).

Desks are identified through their signal-to-noise ratios and intensities only; expected returns
and trading costs enter only through their ratio, and two populations with the same intensity-
weighted distribution of signal-to-noise ratios are observationally equivalent.
(ii) Fix a centres c and let the ratios lie ins c + ∆s[−1,1]⊂[s ,¯s]. Recovering the intensity-
weighted distribution of signal-to-noise ratios to Wasserstein accuracy of order∆s, or its
weightsq iη(si), fromNnoisy reset experiments with worst-case error probability below1/4
requiresN≥cσ 2(∆s)−(4K−2); on separated configurations, Prony’s method of Theorem S4.2
composed withη−1 achieves this order. The constants are those of Theorems S4.1 and S4.2
multiplied by powers ofc − andc +.
Proof.(i) By Lemma S1.1(i) deskiis a group with memory rate 1−η(s i) and weightq i, so
Theorem S2.3 applies, andβ i,λ tc,i enter the economy only throughq i. (ii) On [s ,¯s],ηis aC 1
bijection onto its image withc−≤η′≤c +. Lower bound: take the two economies of Theorem S4.1
with centrer c = 1−η(s c) and rate spacing ∆η=c −∆s. By the mean value theorem their gains are
images of ratios ins c + ∆s[−1,1], so they are two populations of Kalman desks in the class, and
the boundN≥cσ 2(∆η)−(4K−2) =cc −(4K−2)− σ2(∆s)−(4K−2) applies to recovering their weights.
Becauseηisc +-Lipschitz, the Wasserstein distance between the two distributions of ratios is at
least 1/c + times that between the two distributions of rates, which Theorem S4.1 bounds below
by a constant times ∆η=c −∆s. Upper bound: ratios ins c + ∆s[−1,1] with spacing at leastγ∆s
have gains inη(s c) +c +∆s[−1,1] with spacing at leastc −γ∆s, a class of the form required by
Theorem S4.2 with rescaled separationγc −/c+; Prony’s method recovers the rates and weights at
that theorem’s rate, andη −1, which is 1/c−-Lipschitz, maps rate errors to ratio errors.
The private information a supervisor would most like to know, how good each desk’s information
is, is exactly what prices hide. In the worked example the two desks’ ratios differ by a factor of
almost two, and separating them already costs hundreds of thousands of dollars of probing capital.
The proposition takes intensities to vary independently of information quality, because they also
reflect costs and capital. The next proposition asks what happens when the economics ties them
together.
Proposition S23.2(Intensity tied to information quality).Let each desk’s intensity be a known
function of its signal-to-noise ratio,q=φ(s)withφpositive andC 2 on[s ,¯s](for a mean–variance
desk,φ(s)increases withs), and letn i ≥0denote the mass of desks with ratios i (a divisible
population, not an integer desk count). Writes(η)for the inverse gain map andw(η) =φ(s(η))η
for the weight per desk as a function of its gain.
(i)Unknown numbers of desks.The merged measure is ∑iniw(η(si))δ 1−η(si). Every positive
measure with finitely many atoms in[1−η(¯s),1−η(s )]is of this form, withn i =m i/w(ηi)
for an atom of massm i at1−η i. So when the numbers of desks of each type are not
known, tying intensity to information quality removes no economy, and every lower bound of
Proposition S23.1(ii) holds unchanged.
(ii)Known numbers of desks.If then i are also known, the set of economies is smaller, but the
single-versus-pair problem keeps its order: one type at gain¯ηwith2ndesks and two types at
¯η±δwithndesks each have taps that differ bynδ 2∂2η[w(η)(1−η) m]¯η+o(δ 2), so separating
them needsN≥cσ 2δ−4 reset experiments, the order the symmetric pair has when intensities
vary freely.
S-80

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
(iii)Self-confirming configurations.Suppose the ratio each desk perceives is the one implied by the
closed-loop dynamics it trades in, through a mapTfrom configurations(s 1,...,s K)to per-
ceived ratios that is continuous on a box ∏i[s,¯s]and maps it into itself. Then a configuration
withT(s) =sexists, and parts (i)–(ii) and Proposition S23.1 apply at it.
Proof.(i) Desk typeiis a group with gainη i =η(s i), memory rate 1−η i and total weight
niφ(si), so it contributesn iφ(si)ηi =n iw(ηi) to the atom at 1−η i. Sincew >0, any atom mass
mi≥0 is attained withn i =m i/w(ηi). (ii) Writeg m(η) =w(η)(1−η) m; the tap difference is
n[gm(¯η+δ) +gm(¯η−δ)−2g m(¯η)] =nδ2g′′m(¯η) +o(δ2), with ∑m sup|g′′m|2 <∞on gains bounded
away from zero, so the Euclidean distance between the two tap vectors isO(nδ 2) and the Kullback–
Leibler divergence overNexperiments isO(Nn 2δ4/σ2); Le Cam’s two-point argument gives the
bound. (iii) is Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem.
So the barrier does not depend on intensities varying freely: it survives any known link between
intensity and information quality as long as the supervisor does not also know how many desks of
each kind there are, and even then the simplest separation problem keeps itsδ −4 order. Whether
the full (∆η)−(4K−2) exponent survives with known numbers of desks is open. Part (iii) shows
only that self-confirming configurations exist under continuity; it does not derive the mapTfrom
a model of the desks’ learning.
S24 Natural excitation and the cost of ambiguity
Natural price innovations can also separate the pair with zero controlled quantity: an exact finite-
sample test on independent three-step blocks needs orderδ −4 blocks (Online Supplement, Propo-
sition S21.2), the same order as the passive Whittle bound of Theorem S4.4. Non-identification
also has a price on the quote side: when the relevant set of economies includes costlier ones, a
robust dealer widens its quote, which the companion paper analyses (Niroula et al., 2026b); group
non-identification alone does not establish that the worst relevant cost exceeds the informed one.
S25 A worked example: two Kalman desks
To make the orders concrete, consider two desks that track the same trend with Kalman filters
(Lemma S1.1(i)) and differ only in their signal-to-noise ratios:σ 2ζ/σ2ν = 0.09 and 0.16. Their gains
areη 1 = 0.2584 andη 2 = 0.3279, so ¯η= 0.2931 andδ= 0.0348. With equal weights, the economy is
observationally the split pair of Theorem S10.1 around ¯η, and the response difference first appears
at lag two,d 2 =−κ 2qδ2 =−1.55×10 −4 (verified).
A supervisor who wants to tell this market from a single desk with gain ¯ηat 5% error using
a single pulse and the third observation needsA= 2σΦ −1(0.95)/(κ2qδ2) = 212 units of executed
quantity, and, under a 0.5% margin and 4 basis points of fees, single-pulse capital of 18,166 par
units. The pulse is very inefficient: the account-level spectral lower bound of Niroula et al. (2026a),
evaluated at this pair (¯η= 0.2931,δ= 0.0348), gives about 77 par units for the optimal flat probe
(rescaling the ¯η= 0.4 constant byδ−4 alone would give 262, because the constant also depends on
¯η). Converting with the measured Roll noise (factor (0.40/0.01)2), the optimal probe costs roughly
1.2×10 5 USDT and the naive pulse about 3×10 7 USDT. Two desks whose signal-to-noise ratios
differ by almost a factor of two are therefore expensive, but not impossible, to tell apart by probing;
three or more desks are much harder (Theorem S4.1).

S26 A spectral blind spot
Even without identification, could a supervisor monitor risk through eigenvalues or volatility?
Theorem S26.1(Spectral blindness).For any eigenvalues0< λ 2 < λ1 <1, one-step price
variances 1>0and stationary price varianceVlarge enough, there is a family of two-state markets
Jθ =
(
λ1 θ
0λ 2
)
with shock covariancediag(s 1,s 2(θ))such that all members share the eigenvalues,
the one-step price variance and the stationary price variance, while the worst-case transient gain
supk∥e⊤1Jkθ∥grows without bound inθ. Heres 1 is the variance of the fresh price shock given the
full prior state; a forecaster who sees prices only can face additional uncertainty about the hidden
state, and the family is the one with positive couplingθ>0.
Atλ= (0.8,0.7),s 1 = 1 andV= 5, the gain is 1.77, 5.10, 16.95, 50.85 and 169.50 for
θ= 1,3,10,30,100, with stationary variance 5.000 in every case; the cumulative gain rises from
18.3 to 1,667.7. No monitor that depends only on eigenvalues and second moments of the price can
be uniformly safe (Figure S4).
Two clarifications prevent over-reading. First, the group economy of Section S1 is itself non-
normal, yet its price output does not amplify (Lemma S8.1). Large weighted gains can be man-
ufactured by output weights alone: withC w = (1,160), a price-and-memory functional peaks at
160.003 att= 0, which is the static weight, not dynamic growth (Niroula et al., 2026a). Second,
in a network ofMidentical group economies coupled by symmetric nonnegative cross-impact, the
coupled operator stays symmetric in the coordinates of Lemma S8.1; with cross-impact 0.05, the
spectral radius rises from 0.822 (M= 1) to 0.973 (M= 10) while the Kreiss constant stays at one,
as it must for a stable symmetric operator. A stable normal contraction cannot exhibit Euclidean
state-norm transient growth. This does not exclude scalar input-output overshoot relative to initial
impact, which also depends on the input and output maps. The positive-group no-overshoot result
is model-specific; the companion paper studies a depth-depletion mechanism.
Proof of Theorem S26.1.WithJ θ upper triangular the eigenvalues are fixed. The stationary
covariance solvesP=J θPJ⊤θ + diag(s1,s 2), andP 11 is affine ins 2 with positive slope, so for eachθ
a uniques 2(θ)≥0 givesP 11 =VwhenVexceeds the value ats 2 = 0. The one-step price variance
iss 1 throughout, ande ⊤1Jθe2 =θgives sup k∥e⊤1Jkθ∥≥|θ|.

S27 Pricing, evasion and the choice of an audit trail
Proposition S27.1(Conditional endogenous-pricing invariance).Letκ=K(Γ M(κ))on a common
admissible interval, with identical innovation laws, initial-state conventions and other primitives
across economies. HereΓ M(κ)is the specified covariance record at a fixed impact andKis a
maintained dealer rule, not a derived general equilibrium.
(i) If equal merged measures imply the same mapΓ M and the fixed point is unique (or a common
selection rule depends only on that map), equal merged measures give equal selected impacts.
(ii) SupposeKisL K-Lipschitz, each covariance map isL Γ-Lipschitz in impact,L KLΓ <1, and
supκ∥ΓA(κ)−Γ B(κ)∥≤ϵ. For existing fixed points,
|κA−κB|≤ LKϵ1−L KLΓ.(25)
Thus a covariance discrepancy of order(∆η) 2K−1 preserves that order only under these uni-
form conditions.
Limits of the pricing extension.Anonymous flow alone implies neither a covariance-only
dealer objective nor uniqueness or continuity of its equilibrium. Multiple equilibria, threshold quote
changes, private information and inventory-dependent rules are outside this proposition; for the
question of invariance, Theorem S2.1 below covers them. Preserving a price-record testing bound
additionally requires a uniformly Lipschitz observation law with the same nondegenerate noise;
adding anonymous flow is a different statistical experiment and requires its own divergence bound.
We therefore make no unconditional claim that endogenous pricing or additional aggregate-flow
observations preserve the price-only lower bound.
Proof of Proposition S27.1.Equal maps have the same fixed-point set; uniqueness or the
declared common selection yields (i). For (ii), putd=|κ A−κB|. The fixed-point equations and the
two Lipschitz bounds gived≤L K(LΓd+ϵ). Since 1−LKLΓ>0, rearrangement proves the displayed
bound. The scalar inequality is machine checked inp1_endogenous_contraction_sensitivity;
the economic derivation and uniformity of the covariance map are assumptions, not consequences
of that algebra. A discontinuous threshold pricing rule need not preserve a small discrepancy.□
Proposition S27.2(Conditional limits of three evasion arguments).Maintain the exogenous
reporting-noise and valid-instrument assumptions of Proposition S5.2; fix the finite number of linked
class labels and a uniformly stable model family.
(i) For a fixed splitu i,m =α i,mui with known common ownership, independent mean-zero report-
ing errors andα i,m≥α >0, only the lagged-flow column of the population IV matrix scales.
ThusΣ i,m = Σi diag(αi,m,1)anddet Σ i,m =α i,m det Σi. Subject to the remaining uniform
moment and excitation conditions, fixed finitely many consistent sub-account estimates can
be summed: the true identity isw i =∑mwi,m, and the estimated sum has sampling error.
The share restriction impliesM iα≤1. This is not robustness to arbitrarily many vanishing
accounts; a common estimated rate does not identify ownership.
(ii) Assume separately an execution cost lower boundEC ζ≥c ζσ2ζ withc ζ >0and a gross profit
upper boundEΠ≤ Πuniform in the spacing. Camouflage is unprofitable whenc ζσ2ζ > Π. A
variance requirement of order(∆η) −(4K−2) requires an additional additive exogenous-noise

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
estimation model with uniformly controlled instruments. Neither this variance requirement
nor the execution bound is established for arbitrary strategic orders.
(iii) If intensity is scaled toγ iwi while1−r i≥η min >0, thenq i≤γ iwi/ηmin→0asγ i→0.
The other groups remain in the aggregate budget; reducing one contribution does not itself
establish that the ceiling is met.
Economic scope.These are conditional calculations about fixed linked accounts, bounded
execution costs and a persistence class. They do not solve an equilibrium game of evasion. In
particular, endogenous orders can affect prices and invalidate the reporting-error instrument, and
a persistence change can offset a reduction in weight. Adversarial account creation and response
adaptation require further modelling.
Proof of Proposition S27.2.(i) The current-price regressor column is unchanged, whereas
the lagged true flow column scales byα i,m; valid independent reporting noise has zero population
covariance with the instruments. Multiplicativity of determinants gives the stated factor. Summing
shares at leastα givesM iα≤1, checked inp1_sybil_share_count_bound. For a fixed finite
collection the summed estimation errors remain of orderT −1/2 under the maintained joint central
limit assumptions; this asymptotic step is not newly formalized here. (ii) Subtract the cost lower
bound from the uniform gross-profit upper bound. Once the former exceeds the latter, expected net

ConsiderMmarkets, a supervisory cycle of ¯Tperiods, and in each market one algorithm
whose conduct does harmℓ >0 per period until it is attributed. With prices alone, attribution
at the required error level takesT p(∆η) =c p(∆η)−(4K−2) periods; with class-tagged records it
takesT t(∆η) =c t(∆η)−2 periods, the orders of Theorems S4.4 and S5.3 with constantsc p,ct >0.
We take the attribution times to be of exactly these orders, which is a modelling assumption:
Theorem S4.4 gives only a lower bound for prices, and if price-only attribution is slower,Gbelow
is larger and disclosure is chosen at least on the band the proposition describes.T t∝(∆η)−2 is the
rate target; for weights at fixed accuracyT t does not grow as ∆ηshrinks, and the lower edge of the
band disappears. Attribution that would take longer than the cycle does not happen within it. An
audit trail costsFper cycle to operate andcper market to comply with, including any information
leakage. The supervisor’s expected loss is thereforeL price =Mℓmin{ ¯T,T p}without disclosure and
Ldisc =F+Mc+Mℓmin{ ¯T,T t}with it. Write ∆η t = (ct/ ¯T) 1/2 and ∆ηp = (cp/ ¯T) 1/(4K−2) for the
spacings at which tagged and price-only attribution just fit within the cycle.
Proposition S27.3(When an audit trail is chosen).LetK≥2and∆η t<∆η p, and letG(∆η) =
min{ ¯T,T p(∆η)}−min{ ¯T,T t(∆η)}be the attribution delay that disclosure saves.
(i) The supervisor strictly prefers disclosure if and only ifF/M+c<ℓG(∆η).
(ii)Gvanishes for∆η≤∆η t, increases on(∆η t,∆ηp]to its maximumG ∗ = ¯T−T t(∆ηp)>0,
decreases wherever it is positive beyond∆η p, and is negative for∆η >(c p/ct)1/(4K−4), where
it returns toward zero from below. Hence the spacings at which disclosure is chosen form
an interval around∆η p, possibly empty: disclosure is never chosen for algorithms so similar
that even tagged records cannot attribute them within the cycle, nor for algorithms so different
that prices attribute them quickly.
S-84

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
(iii) The interval is non-empty if and only if the audit trail covers more thanM ∗ =F/(ℓG∗−c)
markets (withM ∗ =∞ifℓG ∗≤c), and it widens asMgrows.
Part (i) restates the comparison of losses. Part (ii) says that disclosure is valuable in a middle
band, where algorithms are close enough that prices cannot attribute them within the cycle but far
enough apart that tagged records can; the polynomial gap between the two delays, (∆η) −(4K−2)
against (∆η)−2, is what makes the band wide. Part (iii) is an economies-of-scale result: an audit
trail with a fixed operating cost pays only when it spans enough markets. This illustrates a
possible economy of scale for market-wide reporting; the cost function is stylised, not estimated
from CAT, and the result neither explains CAT’s legal architecture nor shows that it is optimal. A
supervisor that can trade has a second option, active probing, whose cost is the frontier computed in
Niroula et al. (2026a); because that cost grows at the same polynomial order, it shortens price-only
attribution only for well-separated algorithms and leaves the comparison for close ones unchanged.
The model takes the harmℓas given and holds the algorithms’ behaviour fixed across regimes;
neither is modelled here.
Proof of Proposition S27.3.(i)L disc<L price isF+Mc<MℓG. (ii)T p andTt are decreasing
in ∆η, withT t≥ ¯Texactly when ∆η≤∆η t andT p≥ ¯Texactly when ∆η≤∆η p. On ∆η≤∆η t

never reaches a positive level), and the level (F/M+c)/ℓ >0 lies aboveG= 0, so the set where

The first inequality follows from the spectral representation and Parseval’s identity. It is uniform

For the statistical expansion we use the sufficient conditions in Ing and Wei (2005, Section 2,

satisfy the required weighted summability, the preceding polynomial bound gives stability, Gaussian
innovations have a Lipschitz distribution function and moments of every order, and the chosenK T

finite observed history alone need not reconstruct the initial states; no such reconstruction is used
here.
Proof of Proposition 5.5.(a) LetZ t be iid centered Gaussian with variancev >0 and let the public
signal bex t =Z t. At datet, two groups with offsetting exposures submit +βZ t+1 and−βZ t+1, for
β̸= 0. Their orders cancel in the public aggregate, which supplies no information aboutZ t+1. A
noiseless group report supplies it exactly. Public forecast risk isvand tagged forecast risk is zero.
The example specifies information and flows; it does not solve a strategic trading game.
(b) Subtract the known feedback contribution. Under the observation and initialization hy-
potheses, conditioning additionally on price news adds no information about futureU. The two
conditional errors are the independent innovation plusκtimes the corresponding error predicting
Ut+1. Their variances differ by the stated amount. Joint Gaussianity identifies conditional means
with linear projections. Ifu i,t+1 = ∑j≥0αjui,t−j +e i,t+1, with joint-past martingale-difference
innovations and a convergent representation, the conditional expectation of the sum is ∑jαjUt−j
under either information set. No gain then remains. Noisy aggregate reports or unknown feedback
states fall outside this statement.
(c) Takex t+1 =ρx t +ϵt+1, 0<ρ<1, with iid Gaussianϵof variancev ϵ>0, and observe only
pt =x t+ft with independent iid Gaussianft of variancevf >0. An explicit instantaneous-feedback
boundary case hasr= 0,q >0, 0< κq < ρanda=ρ−κq: its current class flowqx t revealsx t.
The price-only observer in this example does not see the signal component observed by that group.
Given the full price history, the steady-state filtering varianceP >0 solves
P= vf(ρ2P+v ϵ)ρ2P+v ϵ +vf.
S-87

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
Without the report, the risk forp t+1 isρ 2P+v ϵ +vf; with the report it isv ϵ +vf. The gainρ 2P >0
comes from separating the current state from observation noise, even though neither group knows
future innovations. This is a counterexample to extending the observed-signal benchmark to every
traded-price observation system.
(d) The hard-limit and intervention claims follow from the cited results with their respective
observation contracts. For smooth saturation, exact identification from noiseless small probes and
finite-sample information under noisy probes are different assertions.
Why the final tag can matter.Once one untagged class remains, its weight can be inferred
from the estimated total and the other estimated weights at a rate independent of the memory
spacing. This does not make an additional independent report valueless. In the Gaussian experi-
mentY=w+e 1 andZ=w+e 2, with independent errors of varianceτ 2, the unbiased estimator
Yhas varianceτ 2, while (Y+Z)/2 has varianceτ 2/2. Both have the same spacing exponent, zero.
The example refutes a universal zero-value claim; correlated or costly reports require their own
comparison.
Proof of Proposition 5.6.For either information set, expandY−f j = (Y−m j) + (mj−fj). The
cross term has conditional expectation zero givenD: first condition onF j, useE[Y−m j|F j] = 0,
and apply the tower property sinceD⊆F j. HenceR j =E[(Y−m j)2|D] +e j. Subtracting
gives the identity, ande B ≥0 gives the upper bound on ∆ ∗. Nested information also implies
∆∗ =E[(m A−mB)2|D]≥0 by the same orthogonality argument. For a concrete limit, letY=C
with unit variance, let the baseline observe no signal, and let the augmented observer seeC. If
both fitted predictors equal zero, their gain is zero while the oracle gain is one. No uniformly valid
inference of oracle irrelevance can follow from that fitted comparison alone.
Numerical evidence and formal scope.The original response-gap simulation and the re-
vised high-precision information calculation are finite-design checks. Neither establishes a uniform
inverse inequality. The earlier forecast simulation chose its window on evaluation loss; it is re-
tained in the source archive as exploratory output and is not used to establish the theorem. The
additional Lean file checks the deterministic polynomial-cancellation, estimation and decision-loss
steps listed in the validation report. The sequential Gaussian likelihood, Pinsker inequality, Gaus-
sian concentration, conditional-projection argument and same-realization autoregression theorem
remain paper-level or cited ingredients. Full mechanization of these results is not asserted.
Recomputed numerical checks for this revision.Table S5 reports the efficient informa-
tion for the first weight after projecting its analytic response derivative off the other weight and
node derivatives. Each design observes 96 response coordinates with unit independent Gaussian
measurement variance. The slope uses type radii 0.1,0.05,0.025, with the family-specific scale in
the script. The smallest-radius computation was repeated at 60 and 90 decimal digits and agreed
to relative tolerance 10−12. These results concern a fixed finite design, not a global inverse theorem
or passive-price likelihood.
A separate stationary simulation fixesL= 16, the two populations, 48 seeds per cell and all eval-
uation choices in advance. A single fitted predictor is evaluated over 1,000 subsequent observations;
excess risk is measured by its error relative to the known conditional mean, avoiding evaluation-
noise subtraction. This is a supporting finite-sample experiment, not the precise growing-order
asymptotic experiment of Theorem 5.2. There is no selection of a window on evaluation loss.

S29 Intervention loss: proof and a two-class construc-

Proof of Corollary 6.2.LetAbe the event that the supervisor authorizes the class-specific halt.
Its equally weighted expected loss is{bP 0(A) +cP 1(Ac)}/2. It is at least min(b,c){1−[P 1(A)−
P0(A)]}/2, hence at least min(b,c)(1−TV)/2. Randomized rules follow by adjoining the same
independent randomization to both experiments. Pinsker’s inequality and nonnegativity give the
second bound. Rearranging it under the KL upper bound proves the sample requirement. This
argument also applies to unequal policy costs; the minimum is a conservative bound, while the
exact Bayes risk is one half the integral of min(bp 0,cp 1) under a common dominating measure.
For the tagged Gaussian experiment, projection of the sample mean on the difference of means
gives a test with both errors Φ(−
√
N∥d∥/(2τ)). The Gaussian tail bound Φ(−z)≤ 12e−z2/2,z≥0,
gives the result. Equal-prior likelihood ratio testing need not minimize cost-weighted loss when
b̸=c, but it supplies the stated upper bound.
Seta= 0.3,κ= 0.5,W= 0.2,r c = 0.5 and ∆ = 0.05. Define the merged measures by nodes
and weights
M0 :r c±∆/
√
3,(W/2,W/2), M 1 : (r c−∆,r c + ∆/3),(W/4,3W/4).
Their moments agree through order two. Convert trading weights to activity byq i =w i/(1−
ri). Both economies haveS≃0.5006<1. After halting the largest class their budgets are
approximately 0.3945 and 0.3455. A ceiling of 0.375 is therefore missed in economy 0 and met
in economy 1. Scaling all activity by 1/3 meets the ceiling in both, with budgets approximately
0.3669. The ceiling is prudential: both original economies are already stable.

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
As ∆↓0, the post-halt budgets converge toa+κW/[2(1−r c)] anda+κW/[4(1−r c)],
respectively. The fixed ceilinga+ 3κW/[8(1−r c)] lies strictly between them. The decision margin
therefore stays positive for all sufficiently small positive ∆, while the public response difference
starts at order ∆3. This explicit example displays theK= 2 mechanism; the all-Kexistence result
is Proposition S12.4. The action presumes a reliable mapping from a class to executable accounts.
Anonymous latent-atom estimation alone does not supply that mapping.
S30 Detection, local-cluster recovery, and uniform log-
loss
This section incorporates the incoming SSRN additions with explicit observation and initialization
conditions. The statements are proved on paper. The revision formal file checks selected supporting
algebra, rather than the full statistical or inverse-map results.
Proof of Theorem 5.1.Condition on the observed history and the next probe. The two next-price
laws have common varianceσ 2 and means differing byd t =κ(F−F ′)(ht). Their conditional
divergence isd 2t/(2σ2). The initial laws and conditional probe laws agree, so the chain rule sums
these terms and gives KL(P T∥P′T ) = ∑t EPd2t/(2σ2). The alternative mean has errorε t+1 +d t,
with expected squareσ 2 +E Pd2t by conditional mean zero. If both errors are below 1/4, their
sum and the total-variation testing inequality give TV(P T,P′T )>1/2. Pinsker then gives KL
greater than 1/2 and Λ T >1. Finally,q= ∑jπjpj≥π ∗p∗ on every path, proving the mixture
inequality. In the two-step counterexample, conditional testing error is Φ(−α|X 1|); its expectation
is the angle probability arctan(1/α)/πfor two independent standard normals. This supplies the
stated counterexample to a universal error curve.
Proof of Theorem 4.7. The response map.Withh 0 =κ, the impulse response satisfiesh t+1 =
aht +κ ∑tm=0kmht−m, so (h 1,...,h L) is a polynomial function of (k 0,...,k L−1), and conversely
kt = (ht+1−aht−κ∑m<tkmht−m)/κ2 gives (k0,...,k L−1) as a polynomial function of (h1,...,h L).
Polynomial maps are Lipschitz on bounded sets. So on any bounded set of kernels there are
constantsC f andC b, depending on (a,κ,L) and the bound, with∥h−h ′∥ ≤Cf∥k−k ′∥and
∥k−k ′∥≤C b∥h−h ′∥.
Lower bound.As in the proof of Theorem 4.5(i), applied to each tap,|k m(ν)−k m(ν′)| ≤
2Wsup|g (2K−1)m |∆2K−1/(2K−1)!, so∥h(ν)−h(ν ′)∥≤C 1∆2K−1. TheNexperiments are Gaussian
with common covariance, so their divergence isN∥h(ν)−h(ν ′)∥2/(2σ2)≤NC 21∆4K−2/(2σ2), and
as in Theorem 5.1 both errors below 1/4 need this to exceed 1/2.
Inverse bound: moments.LetZbe the compact set of parametersz= (s,w) with−1≤s 1 <
···< s K≤1,s i+1−si≥γandw i∈[ω min,ω max], and letµ(z) = ( ∑iwispi )2K−1p=0 . The mapµis
injective onZ: ifµ(z) =µ(z ′), the signed measure ∑iwiδsi−∑iw′iδs′i has at most 2Katoms andintegrates every polynomial of degree below 2Kto zero; choosing the polynomial that vanishes at
all its atoms but one shows that every atom has zero net weight, and since the weights are positive
and the nodes ordered,z=z ′. The Jacobian ofµhas columns (s pi )p and (wipsp−1i )p, a confluent
Vandermonde matrix with determinant± ∏iwi∏i<j(si−s j)4 ̸= 0. An injective continuously
differentiable map on a compact set with nonsingular Jacobian has a Lipschitz inverse: if not, there
would bez n̸=z′n with∥µ(z n)−µ(z ′n)∥/∥zn−z′n∥→0; along a subsequencez n→zandz ′n→z′,
and eitherz̸=z ′, contradicting injectivity, orz=z ′, contradicting the nonsingular Jacobian atz.
So there isc µ>0 with∥µ(z)−µ(z ′)∥≥c µ∥z−z ′∥onZ.

Rank examples.A linear relation ∑p<2Kcpg(p)(θc) = 0 says that the functionalℓ(φ) =∑

in log(m+ 1) vanishes at theL≥2Kdistinct values log 1,...,logL; it is zero.

The incoming simulation evaluates 64 pair-and-probe combinations, local fits initialized at the
truth, and two Bayesian AR examples. Its first experiment truncates memory at 120 taps; it is
a check of that finite simulation model. Its Gaussian error curve is only a reference, and its local
optimizer is not a proof of global computation. Additional regression tests in the revision package
check sequential Gaussian scores against the batch integral at non-unit noise variances, the random-
gap counterexample, the need for zero initialization, and the effect of dropping the rank condition.
The frozen market-forecast calibration failures remain separate from these theoretical examples.
S31 Machine checks in detail
Theorem˜S3.1.Paper1InventoryEquilibrium.leanchecks the algebraic core of part (i):
•the dealers’ first-order condition holds in every state;
•the next price loads on the next flow shock with coefficientκ/D;
•the no-feedback existence condition.
Part (iii) is proved on paper. For part (iv),Paper1InventoryIFT.leanapplies Mathlib’s inverse
function theorem along a one-parameter family of feedbackG=ϵg, withgstrictly differentiable at
D= 1. For all smallϵit gives a zero near (κ −,1), its uniqueness in a fixed neighbourhood, and the
two first-order derivatives in the statement (p1_inventory_small_feedback). The version that is
uniform over all small group measures, in the norm of the statement, is proved on paper.
Theorem S4.1.Lean proves the bound for everyKand every horizonT, with a constant that
does not depend onT. It uses the theorem’s stability hypothesis|a|+|κ|W/(1−r c−∆η)≤ς <1,
S-92

Niroula, Niroula and Khatiwada Observationally Equivalent Algorithms
and holds for all error probabilities withα 0 +β 0≤1 (p1_thm_kcohort_lower_bound_uniform):
4σ2(1−α 0−β 0)2≤NC 2U(∆η)4K−2. The checked pair is a different moment-matched pair with the
same order: binomial weights on 2Kequally spaced nodes, split into odd and even halves. For the
Gauss and Gauss–Radau rules themselves, Lean checks the caseK= 3 (positive weights, matched
moments and the fifth-moment gap). Lean also checks both estimator statements for this pair,
for every horizon (p1_thm_kcohort_lower_bound_weights,p1_thm_kcohort_lower_bound_W1).
The first covers an estimator of the weights, listed in order of memory rate, that is accurate to
LW/4L in each coordinate with probability at least 3/4 under both economies. The second covers
an estimator of the merged measure that integrates every 1-Lipschitz function vanishing at zero to
within 2∆ηW/(π(2K−1)). For measures of equal mass, a Wasserstein error below that accuracy
implies this. Both forceσ 2≤NC 2U(∆η)4K−2, here withL=K−1. The matching lower order
∥hA−hB∥2 ≳(∆η) 2K−1 is proved on paper.
Theorem S4.2.Lean verifies this theorem for everyK≥1, using only Lean’s standard axioms.
The formal proof follows the one above: a Lipschitz bound for the closed-loop deconvolution that
does not depend on ∆η, the (∆η) −m rescaling, a sub-Gaussian tail bound over the 2Ktaps,
injectivity of the Jacobian of the moment map (proved by Hermite interpolation, without the
determinant formula), and a uniform inverse function theorem on the compact class. The formal
estimator is the representation with increasing nodes and nonzero weights that matches the 2K
estimated moments; Lean shows that it is unique and that Prony’s linear system has a unique
solution whose polynomial has exactly its nodes as roots, so the roots are real. The formal statement
allows sub-Gaussian noise and assumes|r c|+ ∆η≤1, so that every memory rate has modulus at
most one.
Theorem S4.3.For everyK, Lean checks part (i) and part (ii) starting from
equal spectra at every frequency (p1 thm minphase zeros,p1 thm minphase spectrum H,
p1 thm minphase spectrum merged). The check usesHin the rational formκzD(z)/((z−a)D(z)−
κN(z)) of Theorem S2.3, andstablein the sense that the poles ofHlie in the open unit disk.
Both are derived from the market recursion. The eigenvalues of the transition of the state
(xt,y 1,t,...,y K,t) are exactly the zeros of the denominator ofH, so Schur stability of the recursion is
the pole condition (p1 eigen iff bottom root,p1 schur stable iff poles in disk). The gen-
erating function of the recursion’s price responseht to a unit trade satisfies
(∑
thtxt)xK+1B(1/x) =
xK+1κx−1D(1/x) as formal power series, whereBis the denominator ofH; withx= 1/zthis is∑
thtz−t =H(z) (p1 impulse generating,p1 tfBottomRev eval,p1 tfTopRev eval).
Theorem S4.4.Lean checks the finite-sample consequence of parts (i) and (iii), not the
divergence-rate statements. In the checked record the market starts from rest and runs for any num-
berMof warm-up periods before theTrecorded prices. The bound does not depend onM, but the
passage to the stationary law (M→∞) is not checked. Part (iii) is checked for every pair of popu-
lations with nonnegative merged weights of total at mostW, normalized rates in [−1,1] and equal
moments below order 2K−1 (p1 thm passive cost matched). This covers theK-group Gauss and
Gauss–Radau economies of Theorem S4.1: both rules are positive with nodes in [−1,1] and inte-
grate polynomials of degree 2K−2 exactly. Lean proves these properties of the two rules forK= 2
andK= 3 (p1 thm passive cost gauss radau two,p1 thm passive cost gauss radau three),
so for theseKthe check covers the paper’s economies with no extra hypothesis. For generalK, the
exactness of the two quadrature rules, a classical property, enters the check as the moment hypoth-
esis. It also covers the binomial pair used in the numerical checks (p1 thm passive cost every K).

S32 Machine-checked declarations
The table lists, for each main result, the Lean 4 declarations that check it and what is proved
only on paper. The inherited project has 1,235 named declarations in 144 modules and builds
against a pinned Mathlib. Its complete local rebuild and strict axiom scan are recorded in this
revision package. The additional revision file is audited separately; counts are not a measure of
manuscript semantic coverage. A full build on 13 November 2026 (Lean 4.34.1, Mathlib commit
d13f23b) passed, together with the axiom audit below; the receipt, which records the SHA-256
hash of every source file, isresearch_extension/current_manuscript_lean_20261008/formal_
verification_receipt.json. An axiom audit of every named declaration finds only Lean’s three
standard axioms (propext,Classical.choiceandQuot.sound). A machine check confirms the
formal statement, not that it matches the prose. The prose statements above say where the two
differ.
